Introduction
This report examines the mathematics results for New Zealand Year 9 students from 
the Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) in 2014/151. 
Along with the reports on New Zealand’s results for mathematics at Year 5, and for 
science at Years 5 and 9, this report forms part of a series of publications about 
New Zealand’s participation in TIMSS 2014/15 (Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016a,b; 
Caygill, Hanlar, & Singh, 2016). International findings for mathematics for TIMSS 
2014/15 have been published by the IEA2 along with a separate international report 
for science (Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 2016; Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016). 
This report examines New Zealand’s mathematics achievement in relation to other 
countries that participated in the study, as well as trends at the Year 9 level from 
1994 to 2014. An examination of the TIMSS assessment questions in relation to 
New Zealand’s mathematics curriculum is presented, followed by analyses of 
achievement by sub-groupings (such as gender and ethnicity) and student 
background factors. Comprehensive coverage of background questions about 
teaching and learning, as well as the school context for learning, is also provided.
1	
Internationally this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 2015. As New Zealand conducted TIMSS towards the end of 2014, it is 
referred to as TIMSS 2014/15 throughout this report.
2	
The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) is an independent, international cooperative 
of national research institutions and governmental research agencies. It conducts large-scale comparative studies of educational 
achievement and other aspects of education.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is a large-scale comparative study of 
mathematics and science achievement at the fourth and eighth grades (Years 5 and 9) around the world. 
As well as examining student achievement, it also monitors curricular implementation and aims to identify 
the most promising instructional practices from around the world.
Conducted on a regular four-year cycle, TIMSS has assessed mathematics and science in 1994/953, 1998/99, 
2002/03, 2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15, with planning underway internationally for 2018/19.
A consortium is responsible for managing the international activities required for the project. This consortium 
consists of: the International Study Center, Lynch School of Education at Boston College, (Massachusetts) 
United States; the IEA Secretariat in Amsterdam, the Netherlands; the IEA’s Data Processing Centre in 
Hamburg, Germany; Statistics Canada in Ottawa, Canada; and the Educational Testing Service (ETS) in 
Princeton, New Jersey in the United States. In New Zealand the Comparative Education Research Unit in the 
Ministry of Education is responsible for carrying out TIMSS.
What value does TIMSS offer?
Although TIMSS is useful for international benchmarking purposes, the real value of TIMSS lies in its ability to 
provide a rich picture of mathematics and science achievement within New Zealand over time.
TIMSS (along with other international assessment studies) can provide information about the performance 
of the New Zealand education system at the national level within a global context. The information from 
studies such as TIMSS is used in the development and review of policy frameworks, and also to inform and 
improve teaching practice. Developments arising out of previous cycles of TIMSS include resource materials 
for schools and teachers, along with teacher in-service training programmes.
What does TIMSS consist of?
TIMSS consists of assessments of students’ achievements in mathematics and science, along with 
questionnaires for students, their teachers, and principals to gather background information. The background 
information provides a context within which the achievement can be examined.
The TIMSS assessments were developed cooperatively with representatives from participating countries. 
Questions were field-tested with a representative sample of students in participating countries, and 
the results generated were used to select and refine the questions for the final test. Questions for the 
background questionnaires underwent a similar process.
Assessment framework for mathematics in TIMSS
As part of the TIMSS methodology, the international consortium develops an assessment framework which 
guides the development of the test items and the context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The 
TIMSS assessments are organised around two dimensions: a content dimension specifying the domains or 
subject matter to be assessed within mathematics and science; and a cognitive dimension specifying the 
domains or thinking processes to be assessed. 
These domains differ for middle primary and lower secondary students to reflect the mathematics curriculum 
taught at each level. The four content dimensions for mathematics at the lower secondary level (Year 9 level 
in New Zealand) are number, algebra, geometry, and data and chance. 
3	
Note that this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 1995 internationally, as most countries participated in 1995. However, southern 
hemisphere countries conducted the assessment towards the end of 1994, so in New Zealand reports the study is referred to as 
TIMSS 1994/95. Similarly for the subsequent cycles, the two years in which administrations occurred in participating countries 
are indicated.

In order to answer questions in the TIMSS test correctly, as well as being familiar with the mathematics 
content, students need to draw on a range of cognitive skills. This is acknowledged in the framework under 
the cognitive domain as:
•
knowing – covers the facts, concepts, and procedures students need to know;
•
applying – focuses on the ability of students to apply knowledge and conceptual understanding to
solve problems or answer questions;
•
reasoning – goes beyond the solution of routine problems to encompass unfamiliar situations,
complex contexts, and multi-step problems.
Exhibit 1 below shows how the TIMSS framework matches to the New Zealand Curriculum and the content 
and cognitive demands that are expected at the lower secondary level (Grade 8 or Year 9 level). Refer to 
Chapter 3 for more information on the framework, especially around the content and cognitive domains.

Who participated?
In TIMSS 2014/15, approximately 270,000 students, 31,000 teachers, and 8,000 schools from 39 countries 
(three of which tested students at a higher grade) and 7 economies (known as benchmarking participants) 
took part at the lower secondary level4.  A list of countries and benchmarking participants at the lower 
secondary level can be found in Exhibit 2. Benchmarking participants will not be referred to in this report.
In this cycle of TIMSS, both Year 5 and Year 9 students from New Zealand participated. Schools in New 
Zealand were sampled so that there was no overlap between the samples. In New Zealand, there were 8,101 
students and 865 teachers from 137 schools assessed at the Year 9 level in October and November 2014.

How was TIMSS administered?
Each lower secondary student was assessed in two timed sessions of 45 minutes, and answered a 
combination of mathematics and science questions. The assessment was a pencil-and-paper test 
containing both multiple-choice and constructed-response questions. Following this, students were given 
a questionnaire containing questions about themselves, their opinions about mathematics and science, 
their computer use, their attitudes to school and leisure activities. Principals and teachers were also given 
questionnaires in order to gain further information about the context in which mathematics teaching and 
learning takes place. In New Zealand, the assessments and questionnaires were conducted in English5.
4	
Some countries only tested students who were much older than the target population. For example, lower secondary students 
should be around 14 years old according to the design of TIMSS (in the eighth grade or the year level where the average age is 
closest to 14). However, in some countries these children have not covered enough of the material to achieve adequately on the 
TIMSS tests, so the country has decided to test much older children. Throughout this report the countries that tested at a higher 
grade and the benchmarking participants are not discussed and do not appear in any totals or comparisons.
5	
In 2002/03, tests and questionnaires were also translated into te reo Māori, but in order to make comparisons between each of 
the cycles, these students were excluded from analyses presented in this report.

What procedures were used to ensure the quality 
of the data?
Members of the consortium ensured procedures were adhered to by all participating countries. TIMSS 
procedures are designed to ensure the reliability, validity, and comparability of the data through:
»
careful planning and documentation;
»
cooperation among participating countries;
»
standardised procedures;
»
attention to quality control throughout.
Procedures included:
»
verification of translations and layout of booklets and questionnaires;
»
monitoring of sampling activities;
»
international and national quality control observers during test administration;
»
checking of data;
»
detailed manuals covering procedures;
»
rigorous training for all involved.
Further information
A lot of information is gathered during the TIMSS administration, and a number of techniques are applied 
when collecting and analysing the data. The Methods and procedures in TIMSS 2015 report (Martin, Mullis, & 
Hooper (Eds.), 2016) contains a detailed account of the assessment framework and instrument development, 
sampling, translation of materials, survey operations, quality assurance, creating the international databases, 
and scaling the achievement data. In addition, when the database is made available, it will be accompanied 
by a guide on how to conduct analysis. Brief details of the technical information are given in the definitions 
and technical notes at the end of this report.
In order to provide a context in which the TIMSS results can be examined, TIMSS also publishes the TIMSS 
2015 encyclopedia: Education policy and curriculum in mathematics and science (Mullis, Martin, Goh, & Cotter 
(Eds.), 2016). This encyclopaedia contains short reports from each country describing mathematics and 
science education policies and practices in that country.

Mathematics achievement 
of New Zealand Year 9 
students since 1994 
– 20 years of trends
CHAPTER 1:
 Key Findings
The average (mean) mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students in 2014/15 was 
statistically the same as that of the students 20 years ago, and not significantly different from 
1998/99, 2002/03, and 2010/11.
The gap between the high and low achievers (range) for Year 9 students was wider in 2014/15 
than in 2010/11, but not as wide as 1998/99. 
Although New Zealand’s mean achievement in mathematics has not changed since 1994/95, 
many other countries have increased.
New Zealand Year 9 students’ mean mathematics achievement was significantly higher than 18 
countries, but lower than the mean score of 17 countries, including all the other predominantly 
English-speaking countries who participated.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9

Mathematics achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
New Zealand’s mathematics achievement in TIMSS can be examined as a snapshot in time compared to 
other countries, but also over time. This first section begins with New Zealand’s achievement in the 2014/15 
cycle of TIMSS. For reporting, the achievement of all participating students has been put on a scale with a 
centre point of 500. This point of 500 was set using the student performance in the 1994/95 cycle of TIMSS, 
and achievement in subsequent cycles was put on the same scale. Achievement was then reported relative 
to this scale using the term scale score points (see Definitions and technical notes for further details on the 
scales and the centre point).
The average (mean) mathematics score for New Zealand Year 9 students in 2014/15 was 493 scale score 
points. New Zealand’s score was significantly lower than the TIMSS scale centre point, but similar to Sweden 
(501), Italy (494), and Malta (494), and higher than 18 countries. However, New Zealand’s mean score (493) 
was lower than the mean score of 17 countries, including all the other predominantly English-speaking 
countries who participated. Scotland, a predominantly English-speaking country with a similar score to 
New Zealand in earlier cycles, did not participate in this cycle.
The highest achieving countries, Singapore and the Republic of Korea, had average achievement among their 
lower secondary students of over 600 scale score points. Of the countries that tested in English, Singapore 
had the highest mean score (621). The next highest mean scores among the countries testing in English were 
a lot lower than Singapore, but higher than New Zealand: Canada (527); Ireland (523); the United States 
(518); and England (518).
It is also useful to look at the range of achievement – the gap between the high and low achievers. The 
lowest outer limit of the bars presented in Exhibit 1.1 is called the 5th percentile, the score at which only 
five percent of students achieved a lower score. The upper-most limit presented is the 95th percentile, the 
score at which only five percent of students achieved a higher score. The range of achievement from the 
5th percentile (345) to the 95th percentile (634) for New Zealand Year 9 students was 288 scale score 
points. New Zealand’s range of achievement was wider than the most of the high-performing countries, 
but narrower than Chinese Taipei (320). In comparison to the English-speaking countries, England (260), 
Ireland (242), and Canada (229) had smaller ranges. Overall, New Zealand’s range for Year 9 students was 
fairly moderate compared with other countries in TIMSS, and more moderate than among New Zealand 
Year 5 students. 
According to the New Zealand Curriculum, studying mathematics and statistics 
equips students with effective means for investigating, interpreting, explaining, 
and making sense of the world in which they live (Ministry of Education, 2007). 
“Improving New Zealand’s economic, social and environmental outcomes through 
growing an innovative society drives the need for an increasingly science, 
technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM)-competent workforce” (Ministry 
of Business, Innovation and Employment and Ministry of Education, 2014). 
In 2014 New Zealand participated in the sixth cycle of TIMSS, a large-scale 
assessment of the mathematics and science skills and knowledge of middle primary 
and lower secondary students. This sixth cycle of TIMSS occurred 20 years after the 
first TIMSS assessment, so we now have information about their achievement over 
this period. Thirty-nine countries, including New Zealand, participated at the lower 
secondary level in this cycle, conducted during 2014 and 2015. This chapter will 
examine the mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students in relation 
to that of other participating countries, and over time.

The range from the 5th to the 95th percentile can move around quite a lot from cycle to cycle. Therefore, it 
was useful to also look at the inter-quartile range, the difference between the 25th and 75th percentile, for 
a more stable version of the range. The inter-quartile range of New Zealand Year 9 students (122) was still 
higher than most of the high-performing countries, but narrower than Chinese Taipei (130).

Exhibit 1.2 provides information to help put mathematics achievement in context. It shows selected structural 
characteristics of education, including expenditure on education, to help the reader understand similarities 
and differences between New Zealand and other participating countries. Countries are presented in the same 
order as Exhibit 1.1 for ease of comparison. The GNI (purchasing power parity) is a measure of income for a 
country, converted to U.S. dollars, and adjusted to allow comparison of real levels of expenditure between 
countries (see http://databank.worldbank.org for details).
Many of the high-achieving countries had much higher income per capita than New Zealand, especially when 
purchasing power was taken into account. The exception was the Republic of Korea, whose GNI was a bit 
smaller than New Zealand, and a lot smaller than the other high-achieving countries. In terms of the countries 
that tested in English, all but Malta had higher income per capita than New Zealand.
Trends in New Zealand’s achievement over 5 cycles of TIMSS
Mean mathematics achievement in 2014 was not statistically different from any of the previous years (see 
Exhibit 1.3). However, as mentioned earlier, New Zealand’s score in this cycle was significantly lower than 
the TIMSS scale centre point, whereas in 1994 it wasn’t. The 2014 result appears to signal a turnaround, with 
a non-significant upward trend observed between 2010 and 2014, but due to this lack of significance, no 
conclusions about an improvement can be drawn.

Trends on the mathematics test questions from 2010/11 
to 2014/15
At the end of each cycle of TIMSS, test questions are released into the public domain. At the beginning of 
the next cycle, new questions are developed to replace released questions. In addition, each cycle of TIMSS 
includes some questions from previous cycles to provide a trend measure over time. This section presents an 
analysis of the trend questions included in both TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15.
There were 127 questions common to both 2010/11 and 2014/15. Of these 127 questions, 33 questions 
had similar proportions of New Zealand Year 9 students correctly answering them across the two cycles 
(as shown in Exhibit 1.5). Just over one-third of the questions (49) showed an increase; that is they were 
correctly answered by more Year 9 students in 2014/15, compared with 2010/11. Similarly, just over one-
third of the questions showed a decrease; that is they were correctly answered by fewer Year 9 students in 
2014/15, compared with 2010/11.
In addition to no overall change in the mean mathematics achievement of Year 9 students between 2010/11 
and 2014/15, these item statistics demonstrate this lack of change.

International trends in mathematics achievement at the 
lower secondary level
New Zealand has now participated in five cycles of TIMSS at the lower secondary level; 1994/95, 1998/99, 
2002/03, 2010/11, and 2014/15 (although offered in 2006/07, New Zealand didn’t participate). The design 
of TIMSS allows us to measure trends over time. Exhibit 1.6 presents changes in mean mathematics 
achievement for those countries that have participated in selected cycles of TIMSS. Lithuania was the 
country with the largest increase in mathematics achievement since the 1994/95 cycle, although most of this 
change happened prior to the 2002/03 cycle. The United States, Hong Kong SAR, the Republic of Korea, 
Slovenia, and England have also had increases of more than 20 score points in mathematics achievement 
since 1994/95. New Zealand, along with Japan and Australia, have had no significant change since 1994/95. 
Norway had a significant decrease between 1994/95 and 2002/03 that has since been reversed.

Relative rankings among countries
Many commentators on the international studies focus on New Zealand’s ranking relative to other countries. 
In order to inform this commentary, Exhibit 1.7 presents New Zealand’s relative ranking in mathematics 
achievement, compared with the other countries who have participated in TIMSS in 1994/95, 2002/03, 
and 2014/15. Of all the 39 countries that participated in TIMSS 2014/15 at the lower secondary level, only 15 
have participated in all these three cycles. In addition, standard errors are presented so that the reader can 
calculate whether apparent differences are real. For example, the score of 527 in Hungary (1994/95) is not 
significantly different from the score of 524 in the Russian Federation (see section entitled Definitions and 
technical notes for details of how significance can be calculated).
Exhibit 1.7 shows that the mean mathematics achievement in New Zealand has been below the mean for the 
15 trend countries in each cycle. In addition, the mean for all 15 countries increased between 1994/95 and 
2014/15. Although there is no significant difference between the mean mathematics score across these three 
cycles, the ranking of New Zealand among these 15 countries is at its lowest in 2014/15, compared to the 
previous cycles.

18
Classifying New Zealand 
Year 9 students as low 
to advanced performers 
CHAPTER 2:
 Key Findings
Six percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were classified as advanced performers (reached 
the advanced benchmark), while 15 percent were classified as below low performers, who did 
not perform simple mathematics tasks. 
Compared with other countries, New Zealand was around the middle when considering the 
proportion of advanced performers, as well as the proportion of lower performers. High 
performing countries had more than one-third of their lower secondary students classified as 
advanced performers, and three percent or fewer classified as below low performers.
The proportions of Year 9 students reaching each classification in 2014/15 were not significantly 
different from 2002/03, or 2010/11. There has been an increase in lower achievers since 
1994/95, with only 11 percent below the low benchmark in 1994/95, compared with 15 percent 
in 2014/15.

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

International mathematics benchmarks
The international benchmarks are four points on the mathematics scale: the advanced benchmark (625 scale 
score points); the high benchmark (550); the intermediate benchmark (475); and the low benchmark (400). 
The performance of students reaching each benchmark is described in relation to the types of questions 
they answered correctly. Exhibit 2.1 presents the 2014/15 descriptions of the international benchmarks of 
mathematics achievement.
Exhibit 2.1: Descriptions of TIMSS 2014/15 international benchmarks of mathematics achievement
Advanced international benchmark — 625
Students can apply and reason in a variety of problem situations, solve linear equations, and make generalisations. 
They can solve a variety of fraction, proportion, and percent problems and justify their conclusions. Students can use their knowledge of 
geometric figures to solve a wide range of problems about area. They demonstrate understanding of the meaning of averages and can 
solve problems involving expected values. 
High international benchmark — 550
Students can apply their understanding and knowledge in a variety of relatively complex situations. They can use 
information to solve problems involving different types of numbers and operations. They can relate fractions, decimals, and percentages 
to each other. Students at this level show basic procedural knowledge related to algebraic expressions. They can solve a variety of 
problems with angles including those involving triangles, parallel lines, rectangles, and similar figures. Students can interpret data in a 
variety of graphs and solve simple problems involving outcomes and probabilities. 
Intermediate international benchmark — 475
Students can apply basic mathematical knowledge in a variety of situations. They can solve problems involving negative 
numbers, decimals, percentages, and proportions. Students have some knowledge of linear expressions and two- and three-dimensional 
shapes. They can read and interpret data in graphs and tables. They have some basic knowledge of chance. 
Low international benchmark — 400
Students have some knowledge of whole numbers and basic graphs. 
Source:	 Exhibit 2.8, Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 2016.
In order to describe more fully what achievement on the mathematics scale means, 
the TIMSS international researchers have developed benchmarks. These benchmarks 
link student performance on the TIMSS mathematics scale to performance on 
mathematics questions, and describe what students can typically do at set points 
on the mathematics achievement scale. These benchmarks classify students by 
the ability they demonstrated in the TIMSS assessment. This chapter describes 
New Zealand Year 9 students’ mathematics achievement using these benchmarks, 
compared to other countries, and over time.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Exhibit 2.2 presents the benchmarks for all countries in two ways. The graphical part shows the proportions 
of students achieving at each of the benchmarks (i.e. above the high benchmark of 550 but not above the 
advanced benchmark of 625). The table part shows the proportions of students achieving at or above each of 
the benchmarks (i.e. at or above the high benchmark of 550, including those above the advanced benchmark). 
Six percent of New Zealand Year 9 students reached the advanced benchmark, the point where students were 
deemed capable of applying and reasoning in a variety of problem situations, solving linear equations, and 
making generalisations. In comparison, more than half the lower secondary students in Singapore, and more 
than one-third of the lower secondary students in Chinese Taipei, the Republic of Korea, Hong Kong SAR, and 
Japan reached this advanced level of mathematics ability. There were also fewer advanced lower secondary 
mathematicians in New Zealand compared with the United States (10%) and England (10%).
Fifteen percent of Year 9 students in New Zealand could not perform many of the simplest tasks TIMSS seeks 
to measure (they correctly completed less than half of the low benchmark tasks). In comparison, only three 
percent or fewer of lower secondary students in the highest-performing countries performed below this low 
benchmark. There were also fewer really low performing lower secondary students (those who did not reach 
the low benchmark) in the United States (9%), England (7%), Canada (4%), and Australia (11%).
Included at the bottom of the exhibit is the international median percentage of lower secondary students at 
each benchmark. The proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students reaching each benchmark was similar to 
the international median percentage, with the exception of the intermediate benchmark.

Changes over time for New Zealand Year 9 students on 
mathematics benchmarks
Since TIMSS began, there has been some concern about the proportion of New Zealand students who have 
low achievement in mathematics. We can use the benchmarks to compare how this proportion has changed 
over time. Exhibit 2.3 presents those Year 9 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown in the 
graphical part) and those students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in the table 
part) in each cycle from 1994 to 2014.
As noted earlier, the range of mathematics achievement has widened since 2002, with the 5th percentile 
getting lower and the 95th percentile getting higher. However, the proportion of Year 9 students reaching 
each benchmark in 2014 was not significantly different from 2002, or 2010. There has been an increase in 
lower achievers since 1994, with only 11 percent below the low benchmark in 1994, compared with 15 percent 
in 2014.
Even though there was not a significant change between 2002 and 2014, the proportion of Year 9 students 
not reaching the low benchmark (15%) remains a concern. These students below the low benchmark did not 
demonstrate basic mathematical knowledge.

23
Strengths and weaknesses 
of New Zealand Year 9
students within 
mathematics
CHAPTER 3:
 Key Findings
At the Year 9 level in mathematics, New Zealand students showed greatest strength at 
statistics questions (called data and chance in TIMSS), compared to algebra and geometry. This 
was a similar pattern to 2010/11.
Year 9 students are also likely to do better at reasoning in mathematics, followed by applying 
their knowledge, while showing less strength at knowing the mathematical concepts, 
procedures, and facts.
There was no change between 2010/11 and 2014/15 across the mathematics content and 
cognitive domains.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

To measure trends across time, some of the items from each cycle are kept secure 
and used in the next cycle. The assessment in 2014/15 had items from the previous 
two cycles, as well as new items. In total there were 212 test questions at the 
lower secondary level in mathematics (Year 9 in New Zealand). As outlined in the 
introductory chapter, the international consortium developed the TIMSS 2014/15 
assessment framework, which guided the development of the test items and the 
context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The assessment framework 
was matched to the curriculum of participating countries to reflect the curricular 
emphasis across countries, and is further explored in Chapter 4. This chapter focuses 
on the strengths and weaknesses of New Zealand Year 9 students in mathematics 
across the two dimensions that make up the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment framework, 
the content and cognitive domains.
Defining the mathematics content and cognitive domains
The TIMSS assessment is designed to cover a wide range of the mathematics teaching and learning 
secondary students around the world are likely to encounter. Like the New Zealand Curriculum, the 
assessment framework is arranged by areas of mathematics, and details the content the assessment will 
cover, termed the content domains. In addition, the thinking processes and behaviours expected as students 
engage with the content range from simple knowledge recall to higher-order thinking skills, termed the 
cognitive domains (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The content domains for mathematics at the secondary level 
are number, algebra, geometry, and data and chance. The cognitive domains are entitled knowing, applying, 
and reasoning.
At the secondary level, the number domain focuses on students’ understandings and skills related to three 
topic areas: whole numbers; fractions, decimals and integers; and ratios, proportion and percent. The algebra 
domain focuses on students’ knowledge about algebraic models and how to use them to solve real-world 
problems, and how to explain relationships involving algebraic concepts. The domain includes three topics: 
expressions and operations; equations and inequalities; and relationships and functions.
The geometry domain focuses on students’ abilities to identify and analyse characteristics and properties 
of two- and three-dimensional geometrical figures. Students at secondary level should be competent in 
geometric measurements like perimeters, areas and volumes, and should be able to solve problems and 
provide explanations based on geometric relationships.
The data display domain has three topics areas: characteristics of data sets; data interpretation; and chance. 
The first two topics focus on students’ ability to read and interpret the meaning of complex visual data 
displays, their understanding of statistics underlying data distributions (e.g. mean, median, mode, and 
spread), and how these relate to the shape of data graphs. The third topic focuses on students’ knowledge of 
fundamental concepts related to probability, like estimating the chances of possible outcomes.
The TIMSS cognitive domain knowing encompasses being able to recall facts, conventions, and concepts, 
along with performing skills such as measuring, classifying, ordering, and computing. Applying mathematics 
knowledge in a range of contexts is the core of the applying domain. The reasoning domain includes 
analysing, synthesising, evaluating, concluding, generalising, and justifying.
The TIMSS questions were not evenly spread across the content and cognitive domains, as shown in Exhibit 
3.1. At the Year 9 level, there was more emphasis on number (31%) and algebra (28%) questions, compared 
to geometry (21%) and data and chance (21%) questions. On the cognitive domains, more emphasis was on 
the questions that required students to apply knowledge (45%), while less emphasis was on the reasoning 
skills (24%).

Mathematics performance in relation to the content and 
cognitive domains
Achievement scores were created for the content and cognitive domains to provide a value so that they 
are comparable with each other. In the early cycles of TIMSS (prior to 2006), a score of 500 on one domain 
was not directly comparable to 500 on another domain. So a new methodology was created and applied to 
questions from the 2006/07 assessment onwards, to ensure that these domains could be compared with each 
other. Therefore, it is important to note that comparisons between domains should only be made between the 
2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15 cycles, but not with earlier cycles (Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013b).

As Exhibit 3.2 shows, New Zealand Year 9 students performed relatively better on number and data and 
chance questions (the difference between these two scores was not significant), compared to algebra 
and geometry. Students also did better at tasks that required them to use their reasoning, and poorer at 
those that required them to demonstrate their knowledge of mathematical concepts, procedures, and facts 
(knowing - see Exhibit 3.3). Applying their knowledge was not significantly different from their performance 
on the other two cognitive areas.
There was no observable pattern of achievement among the high-performing countries across the 
mathematics content domain areas; each country did better in one and poorer in other areas. The only 
country that stood out with uncommon results was Hong Kong SAR, where lower secondary students 
achieved almost similar scores across all content areas. While there was a big variation on the content 
domains, on the cognitive domains, most high-achieving countries had no significant difference in 
achievement across all three areas of knowing, applying and reasoning. Among the countries with variation 
across domains, applying and reasoning were relative strengths in Japan, Canada, Norway and Sweden, while 
Singapore and the United States did better in knowing.

Changes over time in content and cognitive domains
New Zealand did not take part in the assessment of Year 9 students in the TIMSS 2006/07 study. Therefore, 
we are able to look at the trend across two cycles only. In TIMSS 2014/15, the pattern of performance of New 
Zealand Year 9 students on the mathematics content and cognitive domains closely resembled the outcomes 
from the previous cycle in 2010/11. As Exhibit 3.4 shows, there was no significant change in the scores across 
the domain areas.

Among the highest performing Asian countries, a significant decrease in the mean scale score from the 
TIMSS 2010/11 cycle was observed for Chinese Taipei in algebra and geometry, and for the Republic of Korea 
in number and data and chance. Both countries also had lower achievement in the knowing and applying 
cognitive domains, compared to the previous cycle. Singapore achieved higher on number, knowing and 
reasoning, and Hong Kong SAR achieved higher on data and chance, while showing no significant change 
across the other content and cognitive domains. Japan has seen a significant increase across all content and 
cognitive domains since 2010/11. Significant increases across all mathematics content and cognitive domains 
from the previous cycle were also observed for the lower secondary students from Kazakhstan.

TIMSS and the New Zealand 
mathematics curriculum
CHAPTER 4:
 Key Findings
Many New Zealand Year 9 students were working at level 4 of the curriculum by the end of the 
year, rather than level 5. Those students working at higher levels had higher achievement.
When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for New Zealand Year 9 
students, there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when 
analysis reduced the test to contain only those appropriate to New Zealand Year 9 students, 
the average student got less than half the items correct.
New Zealand Year 9 students did best on data and chance questions, followed by number 
questions, the areas of the curriculum most likely to be covered by their teachers.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

TIMSS examines all aspects of curriculum: the intended curriculum; the implemented 
curriculum; and the attained curriculum. In New Zealand, the New Zealand 
Curriculum (NZC) lays out what is intended to be implemented and attained, and 
this guides teaching and learning. 
In the NZC, the alignment of curriculum levels with year levels is flexible. Teachers 
are expected to tailor lessons to meet students’ individual needs (the implemented 
curriculum). Students in the same year level may be working at different curriculum 
levels, as appropriate to their abilities and pace of progression. 
As in previous cycles of TIMSS, teachers were asked to indicate which topics they 
had taught to their Year 9 students in the current school year (the implemented 
curriculum), and the curriculum level the majority of their Year 9 students were 
working at (the attained curriculum). This section will examine the implementation 
of the curriculum, as well as the match between the TIMSS test and the intended 
curriculum.
Mathematics curriculum levels and the TIMSS content 
domains
According to the New Zealand Curriculum, by the end of their ninth year of schooling, students will be 
achieving at early level 5 in the mathematics and statistics learning area of the curriculum (Ministry of 
Education, 2007). New Zealand teachers were asked at which level(s) of mathematics and statistics in the 
New Zealand Curriculum most of their Year 9 students were working for each of the strands: number and 
algebra; geometry and measurement; and statistics. Exhibit 4.1 shows that the proportion of Year 9 students 
working at level 5 of the curriculum varied across strands, and that many classes were working across levels 
of the curriculum. Fewer than ten percent of students were in classes where the majority of students were 
working at level 3 of the curriculum. Just under 30 percent of Year 9 students were working across levels 
4 and 5. Results from the National Monitoring Study of Student Achievement (Educational Assessment 
Research Unit & New Zealand Council for Educational Research, 2015) also show many students working at 
lower levels of the curriculum than intended.
We can use the TIMSS content domains to examine attainment on the curriculum strands due to their 
similar mathematical content. For example the number and algebra content domains for lower secondary 
in TIMSS consist of understandings and skills related to six topic areas: whole numbers; fractions, decimals 
and integers; ratios, proportion and percent; expressions and operations; equations and inequalities; and 
relationships and functions (see Gronmo, Lindquist, Arora, & Mullis, 2013). Similarly the number and algebra 
strand at level 3 of the NZC consists of: number strategies and knowledge (which includes fractions, 
decimals, integers, ratios, and percentages); equations and expressions; and patterns and relations (Ministry 
of Education, 2007). 
Exhibit 4.1 shows that students whose classes were working at higher levels of the curriculum had higher 
achievement on the associated TIMSS content domain. Examining these results in the international context 
shows that if we had only included those Year 9 students working at level 5 of the curriculum in the TIMSS 
testing, New Zealand would have had a mean score similar to the high-performing countries (Singapore, 
the Republic of Korea, Hong Kong SAR, Chinese Taipei, and Japan). For example, the average score for 
New Zealand Year 9 students working at level 5 of the statistics strand of the curriculum was the same on the 
corresponding data and chance content domain (600) as their Korean counterparts (600 scale score points). 
However, the score for those New Zealand Year 9 students whose classes were working across levels 4 and 5 
on the data and chance domain (558) was a lot lower than the high-performing countries.

Curriculum match
Questions about international studies often focus on the appropriateness of the assessment questions 
for New Zealand students. New Zealand is not unique in asking this question; other countries are also 
concerned with the appropriateness of these tests. The TIMSS assessment questions are developed through 
a collaborative process that begins with the development of an assessment framework. The TIMSS 2015 
mathematics framework (Gronmo, Lindquist, Arora, & Mullis, 2013) was designed to specify the important 
aspects of mathematics that participating countries agreed should be the focus of an international 
assessment of mathematics achievement. However, it is inevitable that the tests included questions that were 
unfamiliar to some students in some countries. In order to investigate the extent to which the TIMSS 2014/15 
assessment was relevant to each country’s curriculum, TIMSS conducted a Test-Curriculum Matching Analysis 
(TCMA). The TCMA was also used to investigate the impact of selecting only appropriate questions on a 
country’s performance.
For the TCMA, each assessment question was examined using the following two criteria:
»»
whether or not the topic of the question is in the intended curriculum for the majority (50% or more) of 
lower secondary students in the grade or school level tested (in our case Year 9);
»»
whether or not the item topic was intended to be encountered by the students prior to the TIMSS 
testing (in our case October or November 2014).
All questions, regardless of this analysis, were included in any overall results reported for TIMSS. However, 
this analysis was used to ascertain the level to which the results might change for New Zealand if only 
questions judged appropriate for New Zealand students were included in the tests. The analysis also included 
an examination of how students in other countries would fare if they were only given the “New Zealand-
appropriate” test.
Exhibit 4.2 shows the proportion of questions considered appropriate to the New Zealand Curriculum in each 
of the TIMSS content areas. However, it should be noted that New Zealand’s mathematics curriculum provides 
some challenges for deciding whether or not at least half of Year 9 students are likely to have met the 
question topics in the TIMSS test. The curriculum is not prescriptive, instead providing some broad guidelines 
of mathematics concepts and skills that schools can choose to cover. Schools are encouraged to design 
mathematics programmes that are relevant to their students and communities. Consequently, when schools 
plan their mathematics programmes there is considerable variation between them. Another challenge is that 
the broad achievement objectives are grouped in levels that cover approximately two years of schooling. 
As shown in the previous section, New Zealand Year 9 students were generally working at levels 4 and 5 of 
the curriculum, so information from levels 4 and 5 was used to guide judgements on the TCMA. In addition, 
curriculum-matched resources available on nzmaths.co.nz were used for further clarification (our thanks to 
the teachers who did this work).
Exhibit 4.2: Appropriateness of the TIMSS 2014/15 tests to the New Zealand Curriculum

As Exhibit 4.2 shows, 80 percent of the TIMSS 2014/15 questions were judged appropriate for New Zealand 
Year 9 students in terms of the curriculum expectations. However, the TCMA analysis shows that even if 
the TIMSS test was reduced to include only those questions considered appropriate to the New Zealand 
Curriculum, the average New Zealand Year 9 student would have got less than half the items correct (see 
Exhibit 4.3). In contrast, the average lower secondary student in some of the high-performing countries 
would have got around three-quarters of the items correct.

Coverage of mathematics topics
To ascertain the implemented curriculum, teachers of the selected students were asked what opportunities 
students had to learn the content in the TIMSS tests. Teachers provided information on mathematics topics 
taught to Year 9 students prior to, or during, the year of the TIMSS assessment. For each of 20 topics, 
teachers were asked if the topic was mostly taught before this year, mostly taught this year, or not yet 
taught or just introduced. These 20 topics cover the TIMSS 2015 mathematics framework (Gronmo, Lindquist, 
Arora, & Mullis, 2013), and they provide a link between the TIMSS framework and the TIMSS assessment. 
Note that this question is about whether the topic was taught, but does not gather information on the 
depth or breadth of the teaching on the topic. For example, the topic of simplifying and evaluating algebraic 
expressions might cover quite a variety of difficulties of expressions in one classroom, but only very simple 
ones in another classroom – this question does not gather that level of detail. 
Three-quarters of New Zealand Year 9 students had been taught all these topics in 2014 or the preceding 
years. Similarly, just over three-quarters of lower secondary students (76%), on average, across all countries 
had been taught all of these 20 topics (ranging from 60% in Slovenia to 90% in the United States – see 
Exhibit 9.4, Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 2016).
More New Zealand Year 9 students had been taught number topics (87%), or data and chance topics (76%), 
in 2014 or the preceding years than algebra topics (69%), or geometry topics (69%). Note that this question 
was not about the proportion of time spent on these, but rather whether the topic was covered or not. 
As mentioned earlier, data and chance, followed by number, were the areas of TIMSS where New Zealand 
Year 9 students showed the best performance, while the other two areas were lower.

»»

In the previous cycle of TIMSS in 2010/11, the topics of simultaneous (two variables) equations (10%), and 
congruent figures and similar triangles (42%) were also the least taught topics in New Zealand. New Zealand 
Year 9 students who were in classes where the topic was covered (the year of the assessment or in previous 
years), generally had higher achievement than those where the topic was not covered.

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
35
Mathematics achievement 
of Year 9 boys and girls 
CHAPTER 5:
 Key Findings
Year 9 boys and girls had the same mathematics achievement as each other, on average, but 
boys had a wider range than girls.
Boys’ average mathematics achievement has not changed much since 1994/95. Girls had a 
significant decrease between 2002/03 and 2010/11, followed by a significant increase between 
2010/11 and 2014/15 – back to the same level as 2002/03.
Similar proportions of Year 9 girls and boys reached each of the mathematics achievement 
benchmarks in 2014/15. More girls reached the high and advanced mathematics achievement 
benchmarks in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. 
Boys and girls had similar achievement, on average, across all mathematics content domains 
(number, algebra, geometry, and data and chance), and all cognitive domains (knowing, 
applying and reasoning).
Year 9 girls made significant progress on almost all mathematics content and cognitive 
domains in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11.  There has not been a significant change in Year 9 
boys’ mathematics achievement since 1994/95.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

New Zealand has many observed differences in achievement across subjects 
between boys and girls, with girls generally outperforming boys (Chamberlain, 2011; 
New Zealand Qualifications Authority, 2011; New Zealand Qualifications Authority, 
2014). This pattern of girls out-performing boys has also been observed in other 
countries, with the exception of mathematics and science, where in many cases boys 
achieve just as well as girls (Bramley, Vidal Rodeiro, & Vitello, 2015). A Ministry of 
Education synthesis (Ministry of Education, 2007) highlighted several issues around 
the achievement of boys which included:
»»
early problems in reading;
»»
lower achievement in reading and writing; 
»»
disengagement with school; 
»»
lower qualification attainment.
The synthesis report was supported by a range of qualification and participation 
data, showing different proportions of boys and girls having success. The previous 
cycle of TIMSS was the first to see a significant difference in mathematics 
achievement between New Zealand boys and girls at the Year 9 level, in favour of 
boys. This chapter will examine the mathematics achievement of Year 9 boys and 
girls in TIMSS in 2014/15, with some comparisons with previous cycles.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Mathematics achievement of boys and girls
As Exhibit 5.1 shows, trends in the mean mathematics achievement for New Zealand Year 9 girls and boys 
had a slightly different trajectory across the cycles. New Zealand Year 9 boys’ mathematics achievement 
has remained similar (statistically) since 1994/95; the 18 score point drop between 1994/95 and 1998/99 was 
not statistically significant. New Zealand Year 9 girls’ achievement, on the other hand, didn’t change much 
between 1994/95 and 2002/03, but significantly dropped between 2002/03 and 2010/11, and increased back 
again between 2010/11 and 2014/15 – back to the same level as 2002/03. 
Exhibit 5.1: Trends in mathematics achievement for New Zealand Year 9 girls and boys in TIMSS from 
1994/95 to 2014/15

The 2014/15 cycle of TIMSS found Year 9 girls and boys had a similar score on mathematics, on average 
(494 and 491 score points respectively). The range of achievement for boys was wider than for girls, as 
shown in Exhibit 5.2. This pattern is consistent with nearly all the previous cycles of TIMSS, where there was 
no significant difference in average (mean) mathematics achievement (except for 2010/11) between boys and 
girls, but a wider range of achievement for boys than for girls. In 2010/11, the mean mathematics achievement 
of girls was significantly lower than boys. With the increase between 2010/11 and 2014/15 for girls, the 
difference between boys and girls disappeared.
There hasn’t been a consistent increase or decrease in the range of achievement for New Zealand Year 9 girls 
and boys since 1994/95. The TIMSS 2002/03 results remain the narrowest for both genders. Between 2010/11 
and 2014/15, the range increased for both girls and boys. For girls, the change was primarily at the top of the 
range, but for boys there was a change at both ends of the spectrum. 
In TIMSS 2014/15, the high-performing countries had no gender differences, with the exceptions of 
Singapore, which had a significant gender difference in favour of girls, and the Russian Federation and 
Canada, in favour of boys.

Benchmarks for boys and girls
As shown in Exhibit 5.3, there were slightly different proportions of New Zealand Year 9 boys and girls 
reaching each benchmark in 2014/15, even though these differences were not significant. There were 
relatively large proportions of both boys (16%) and girls (14%) who did not reach the low benchmark — these 
Year 9 students did not demonstrate the ability to complete the basic mathematics tasks that TIMSS seeks to 
measure (see Chapter 2 for more information on benchmarks).

Achievement on the content and cognitive domains for boys 
and girls
Similar to the general pattern of achievement between Year 9 girls and boys, there were no overall 
differences in mean mathematics achievement between them across all content and cognitive domains in 
TIMSS 2014/15. As the Exhibit 5.5 shows, this is different to the 2010/11 results, where boys achieved higher 
on the number, geometry and applying domains, compared to girls.
Compared to TIMSS 2010/11, Year 9 girls made significant progress on all domains, except algebra and data 
and chance, in TIMSS 2014/15. Year 9 boys’ achievement didn’t see significant differences across any of the 
content or cognitive domains between the two cycles.
Internationally, girls achieved higher than boys, on average, in algebra, geometry and data and chance. 
However, the pattern varied across countries. The high-achieving countries had girls doing better in algebra, 
while having mostly similar scores with boys on geometry and data and chance.

41
Mathematics achievement, 
ethnicity, and language of 
the home of Year 9 students 
CHAPTER 6:
 Key Findings
One-fifth of all Year 9 students identified themselves as belonging in more than one ethnic 
grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasifika, Pākehā/European, Asian, and Other.
There was a wide variation in mathematics achievement within ethnic groupings, with 
advanced achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings.
Māori had lower average mathematics achievement than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasifika Year 9 
students had lower average mathematics achievement than non-Pasifika. There was evidence 
that this achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken into account.
Between 2010/11 and 2014/15, there was a significant increase in average mathematics 
achievement for Pākehā/European Year 9 girls.
One in five Year 9 students regularly spoke a language other than English at home (most also 
spoke English).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

In the four year plan of the Ministry of Education, one of its stated priorities is “Māori 
and Pasifika learning and success” (Ministry of Education, 2016). They state that 
“Equitable achievement for Māori, Pasifika and students from low socio-economic 
backgrounds is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of 
Education, 2016). While Māori and Pasifika are not a new focus, with previous 
documents and programmes aimed towards these students, it is important to review 
our progress towards this goal. Given that Māori and Pasifika will be an increasingly 
large proportion of New Zealand’s working age population in the future, improving 
the system so that more of them will achieve will contribute to the future wellbeing 
and prosperity of New Zealand (Ministry of Education, 2016).
It is important to interpret this data carefully. It is not the ethnicity of a student, per 
se, that influences outcomes. However there is clear evidence that when teaching is 
culturally responsive, Māori students, and those of other minority ethnic groupings, 
thrive at school (Bishop et al., 2007). In contrast, Blank, Houkamau, & Kingi (2016) 
suggest that the unconscious bias of teachers lowers their expectations of what 
Māori can achieve. Thus the existence of a relationship between ethnicity and 
achievement, demonstrated in this section, does not imply that being classified in 
a particular ethnic grouping is a cause of poor or good achievement. In contrast, 
language knowledge may strongly influence mathematics learning, with languages 
like Māori, and some of the Asian languages, being inherently structured for an 
understanding of base ten and place value. 
This section will examine mathematics achievement among ethnic groupings and 
language users. Students were able to identify with more than one ethnic grouping, 
and so results should be read with this overlap in mind. Also note that only English-
medium students are included in this cycle of TIMSS.
42
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Classifying students by ethnicity
Students were asked about their ethnicity by asking “are you...?” and then listing the major ethnic groupings 
in New Zealand. The list also included an opportunity for the student to record any Other grouping they 
might identify with. In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand classified each student to one grouping only, 
using prioritisation. In this cycle, any student who identified with more than one grouping was assigned 
to each of those groupings, in line with the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classification (Statistics 
New Zealand, 2005). Students were then assigned to the following groupings based on their responses: 
Māori; Pasifika; Pākehā/European; Asian; and Other. If a student ticked both Māori and Samoan, they would 
be classified as both Māori and Pasifika. Using this methodology, ethnic groupings cannot be compared 
with each other, as 20 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students are in more than one grouping (some are 
in as many as four groupings). Exhibit 6.1 shows how students were classified. There were proportionately 
more Year 9 students in each ethnic grouping using this methodology than if we had used prioritisation.
Exhibit 6.1: Proportion of Year 9 students classified in each ethnic grouping in TIMSS 2014/15

Proportion of Year 9 students

The next sections examine the mathematics achievement of Year 9 students within each ethnic grouping, 
and comparisons are only made with those that are NOT in that ethnic grouping.
Mathematics achievement among Māori
As shown in Exhibit 6.2, there was a range of achievement among Māori Year 9 students. The difference 
between high and low achieving Māori was almost the same as non-Māori. However, the whole distribution 
for Māori was lower than for non-Māori, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of Māori was much 
lower than non-Māori. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS, and other studies.

Mathematics achievement among Māori girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the mathematics achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, 
Māori Year 9 girls had the same mathematics achievement as Māori Year 9 boys. This finding was different 
from 2010/11, when Māori boys had higher achievement than Māori girls. Between 2010/11 and 2014/15, there 
was a small, non-significant increase for Māori Year 9 girls, and a small, non-significant decrease for Māori 
Year 9 boys, reducing the difference.
Mathematics achievement among Māori students, taking into account socio-
economic status
Some preliminary analyses were conducted to see if taking into account the socio-economic status of 
students affected the achievement of Māori students. Fewer households are buying and storing lots of 
books as the popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases (see chapter 7). However, the number of 
books in the home still has a strong relationship with achievement. These preliminary analyses included only 
books in the home and home possessions, but show that when these measures of socio-economic status are 
taken into account, the difference in achievement between Māori and non-Māori is smaller (35 scale score 
points instead of 56). This finding suggests that ‘being Māori’ does not explain the difference in achievement 
between Māori and non-Māori.  Further exploration is warranted to understand the difference in achievement 
between Māori and non-Māori. An understanding of the barriers to learning can help break those barriers 
down, so that Māori students can achieve to their true potential. Further exploration should also keep in 
mind the idea that Māori students face impediments to learning due to deficit thinking by teachers (see, for 
example, Blank et al., 2016).
Mathematics achievement among Pasifika
As shown in Exhibit 6.6, there was a range of achievement among Pasifika Year 9 students. The difference 
between high and low achieving Pasifika was a little smaller than for non-Pasifika. However, the whole 
distribution for Pasifika was lower than for non-Pasifika, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of 
Pasifika was lower than non-Pasifika. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS, and other studies.

As mentioned earlier, the TIMSS benchmarks provide an understanding of achievement beyond mere averages

students that were high achieving (at or above the high benchmark – see Exhibit 6.8). In comparison, nearly 
one-third of non-Pasifika Year 9 students achieved at or above the high benchmark.
Nearly one-third of Pasifika Year 9 students did not demonstrate the ability to complete a reasonable 
number of the simplest mathematics tasks that TIMSS seeks to measure (that is they did not reach the low 
benchmark). As shown in Exhibit 6.8, there were far fewer non-Pasifika who were very low achievers (12% 
below the low benchmark), compared with Pasifika.

Mathematics achievement among Pasifika girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the mathematics achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, 
Pasifika Year 9 girls had the same mathematics achievement as Pasifika Year 9 boys.
Mathematics achievement among Pasifika students, taking into account socio-
economic status
Some preliminary analyses were conducted to see if taking into account the socio-economic status of 
students affected the achievement of Pasifika students. Fewer households are buying and storing lots of 
books as the popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases (see chapter 7). However, the number 
of books in the home still has a strong relationship with achievement. These preliminary analyses included 
only books in the home and home possessions, but show that when these measures of socio-economic 
status are taken into account, the difference in achievement between Pasifika and non-Pasifika is smaller 
(34 scale score points instead of 65). This finding suggests that ‘being Pasifika’ does not explain the 
difference in achievement between Pasifika and non-Pasifika. Further exploration is warranted to understand 
the difference in achievement between Pasifika and non-Pasifika. An understanding of the barriers to 
learning can help break those barriers down, so that Pasifika students can achieve to their true potential. 
Further exploration should also keep in mind the idea that Pasifika students face impediments to learning 
due to deficit thinking by teachers (Bishop et al., 2007).
Mathematics achievement among Pākehā/European
As shown in Exhibit 6.10, there was a range of achievement among Pākehā/European Year 9 students. 
The difference between high and low achieving Pākehā/European was smaller than non-Pākehā/European. 
However, the whole distribution for Pākehā/European Year 9 students was higher than for non-Pākehā/
European, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of Pākehā/European is higher than non-Pākehā/
European. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS, and other studies.

Mathematics achievement among Other girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the mathematics achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Although 
there weren’t many students in this ethnic grouping, so this result should be read with caution, Other Year 9 
girls had higher mathematics achievement than Other Year 9 boys. 
Mathematics achievement by language
Students were asked how often they spoke the language of instruction at home. In New Zealand, TIMSS 
was only tested in schools where English was the main medium of instruction. This question was formulated 
differently from previous cycles, so no comparisons can be made with these previous cycles. Nearly 80 
percent of Year 9 students (79%) indicated that they always spoke English at home, and another 14 percent 
almost always spoke English at home. Nearly all of the rest of the Year 9 students indicated that they 
sometimes spoke English and sometimes another language (6%). Less than one percent of Year 9 students 
reported never speaking English at home. Those students who spoke another language, at least a little, most 
commonly spoke an Asian language or Māori every day at home. 
Those Year 9 students who sometimes or never spoke English at home most commonly spoke an Asian 
language or a Pasifika language, as shown in Exhibit 6.22.

Many studies point to the advantages of bilingualism (or indeed multilingualism), including greater flexibility 
of thinking (see, for example, Adesope, Lavin, Thompson, & Ungerleider, 2010). The base 10 structure of 
languages such as Māori and Chinese may also be an advantage for students learning to add and subtract. 
The relationship between language used at home and average mathematics achievement in the English-
medium TIMSS test was complex. Because the mathematics questions in TIMSS include many items that 
require students to first read the question and work out which mathematics is required, a good facility with 
English is required. Therefore, to fully understand the relationship between being bilingual, or multi-lingual, 
and mathematics achievement, we need to know how well the students understand each language.

54
Mathematics achievement 
and socio-economic status 
of Year 9 students  
CHAPTER 7:
 Key Findings
Students in homes with many resources for learning had higher mathematics achievement, on 
average, than those whose homes had fewer resources.
Mathematics achievement was higher, on average, for Year 9 students in schools with more 
affluent students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The 
difference in mathematics achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was 
a lot higher than the international average.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

The Ministry of Education’s four year plan from 2016 to 2020 states that “Equitable 
achievement for Māori, Pasifika and students from low socio-economic backgrounds 
is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Results from the large-scale international studies, PIRLS, TIMSS, and PISA1, have all 
shown New Zealand has a large disparity in achievement between students with 
high socio-economic status (high SES), and those with low socio-economic status 
(low SES). Schools in New Zealand have received differential funding, based on the 
needs of the communities they are situated in, since TIMSS first began (in 1994), yet 
disparities persist. Therefore, it is important to continue to examine the relationship 
between socio-economic status and achievement of students. This chapter will 
present details of some measures used to examine socio-economic status, along with 
their association with mathematics achievement.
Possessions and books as measures of socio-economic 
status of students
Home possessions and books in the home can be used to give a measure of both the wealth of the home 
and the level of importance given to education and culture. The TIMSS questionnaires asked lower secondary 
students about the presence in their home of books, and a series of other resources which could be used to 
gauge SES. Arguably, some of these resources could be considered facilitators of educational activities, such 
as an internet connection, but might not necessarily be used as such. This next section will discuss the results 
of these questions.
Books in the home
TIMSS has asked about the number of books in the home since 1994/95. Exhibit 7.1 shows the proportions 
of students in three summarised categories of numbers of books in the home, and their average (mean) 
mathematics achievement.
One-third of New Zealand Year 9 students (33%) reported that they had 25 or fewer books in their home. 
Just over one-third of New Zealand Year 9 students (37%) reported that they had more than 100 books in 
their home. This proportion of students with more than 100 books is similar to 2010/11 (40%), but much lower 
than in 1994/95 (66%). It seems likely that fewer households are buying and storing lots of books as the 
popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases.
As shown in Exhibit 7.1, Year 9 students that reported more books in the home had significantly higher 
mathematics achievement, on average, than those with fewer books. This implies that despite fewer 
households buying and storing lots of books, the number of books in the home still has a strong relationship 
with achievement.
 1	
PIRLS is the Progress in International Reading Literacy Study, and has been conducted in 2001, 2005, 2010, and 2015 in New 
Zealand – see http://www.educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls.
	
PISA is the Programme for International Student Assessment, and has been conducted in 2000, 2003, 2006, 2009, 2012, and 
2015 in New Zealand – see http://www.educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa.

More Pākehā/European students had many books in their home (more than 100 books – 45%) than non-
Pākehā/European students (17%). Book ownership was less common among Pasifika students (17% had more 
than 100 books), Asian students (29% had more than 100 books), and Māori students (24% had more than 
100 books). Despite there being fewer Year 9 students in some ethnic groupings, the strong relationship 
between books in the home and mathematics achievement was still evident within each ethnic grouping.
Resources in the home
Exhibit 7.2 shows the proportions of Year 9 students that had each of the listed resources in their homes. 
Nearly all students reported having a computer in their home (97% had either a computer of their own, or a 
computer they shared with others in their family), and the majority of students had an internet connection 
(93%). Note that adults who supervised the questionnaire administration regularly reported receiving queries 
about how to answer this question from students who lived in more than one house, as part of a shared 
custody arrangement.

Socio-economic indicators of schools attended
Schools with larger numbers of students from low socio-economic communities are provided with extra 
funding per student. The decile indicator within New Zealand is used to allocate differentiated funding. 
Decile 1 schools are the schools with the highest proportion of students from socio-economically 
disadvantaged communities, while decile 10 schools have the lowest proportion of students from these 
communities. Internationally, there was also information collected from principals that allowed examination of 
the socio-economic status of the school intake. This section will examine these differing measures of socio-
economic status: decile; and composition by student economic background.

Decile
Previous cycles of TIMSS have shown that students attending schools with fewer students from lower socio-
economic backgrounds (having higher decile) had higher average mathematics achievement than those 
attending schools with more students from lower socio-economic backgrounds (from lower decile schools – 
see Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013). As shown in Exhibit 7.5, this was also true of the latest cycle of TIMSS, 
with students from higher decile schools (9 and 10) having higher average mathematics achievement (536 
scale score points) than those from the low decile schools (1 and 2 – 422 scale score points).

The TIMSS benchmarks provide an understanding of achievement beyond mere averages and ranges (see 
Chapter 2 for details of these benchmarks). Exhibit 7.6 presents two different ways of looking at this data –

data, as presented in the international reports.
There were high achieving Year 9 students in all decile groupings, as measured by the high and advanced 
benchmarks (see Exhibit 7.6). However, there were larger proportions of Year 9 students in the higher decile 
schools achieving at or above the high benchmark (44% in decile 9 and 10 schools), compared with the 
lower decile schools (7% in decile 1 and 2 schools). As shown in Exhibit 7.6, there were Year 9 students in 
all decile groupings who did not demonstrate the ability to complete a reasonable number of the simplest 
mathematics tasks which TIMSS seeks to measure (that is they correctly completed less than half of the low 
benchmark tasks). However, there were larger proportions of Year 9 students in the lower decile groupings 
in this very low achieving group (39% of decile 1 and 2 students below low), compared with high decile 
groupings (5% of decile 9 and 10 students).

School composition by student economic background
To make international comparisons across schools, there was also information collected from principals 
across the TIMSS countries that allowed examination of the socio-economic status of the combined student 
population in the schools. The School Questionnaire included two questions, one that asked about the 
approximate proportions of students in the school from economically disadvantaged homes, and one that 
asked about the approximate proportions of students from economically affluent homes. Principals were 
asked to choose from four categories: 0 to 10%; 11 to 25%; 26 to 50%; and more than 50%. The international 
researchers combined the responses from these two questions to give a measure of school composition by 
student economic background. The measure created was an index with three categories: schools with more 
affluent than disadvantaged students; schools with neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged students; 
and schools with more disadvantaged than affluent students. This measure allows us to compare the equity 
of our system with other countries.
As shown in Exhibit 7.7, nearly one-third of New Zealand Year 9 students (30%) were in schools with more 
affluent students, while just over one-quarter (28%) were in schools with more disadvantaged students. 
Average mathematics achievement was higher for Year 9 students in schools with more affluent students 
(529 scale score points), than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students (449 scale 
score points). The difference in mathematics achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand 
(81 scale score points) was higher than on average internationally (56). South Africa (134), Singapore (106) 
and England (89) had higher differences than New Zealand, while Australia (71) and the United States (64) 
had lower differences between the lower secondary students in more affluent schools and those in more 
economically disadvantaged schools.
This large difference in average mathematics achievement between lower secondary students in schools 
with more affluent students, compared with schools with more disadvantaged students, was consistent with 
TIMSS 2010/11.

As with the measure of decile, the TIMSS benchmarks show that there are high and low achievers within 
each of these three categories of economic composition of the school population. Exhibit 7.8 presents two 
different ways of looking at this data – those Year 9 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown 
in the graphical part) and those Year 9 students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in 
the table part).
There were larger proportions of Year 9 students in the more affluent schools achieving at or above the high 
benchmark (40%), compared with the more economically disadvantaged schools (13% of Year 9 students). 
As shown in Exhibit 7.8, there were Year 9 students in each of the three categories of school composition 
who did not demonstrate the ability to complete a reasonable number of the simplest mathematics tasks 
which TIMSS seeks to measure (that is they did not reach the low benchmark). However, there were larger 
proportions of Year 9 students in the more economically disadvantaged schools in this low achieving group 
(30%), compared with the more affluent schools (4%).
