Life outside of school for 
Year 9 students learning 
mathematics  
CHAPTER 8:
 Key Findings
About 60 percent of Year 9 students read for enjoyment daily. Those students that read books, 
e-books or comics for enjoyment more often had significantly higher average mathematics 
scores than those who read less often.
Overall, more Year 9 girls read for enjoyment daily than boys. Within ethnic groupings, fewer 
Māori and Pasifika Year 9 students read daily than non-Māori and non-Pasifika Year 9 students. 
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely to go to the movies and participate in various 
outdoor activities than other leisure activities. Doing leisure activities did not appear to have a 
relationship with student achievement.
New Zealand Year 9 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity, compared 
to other regular activities. Overall, increased time spent on regular out of school activities was 
associated with lower mean mathematics achievement.
Tutoring in mathematics was less common in New Zealand than in many countries, nine percent 
of New Zealand Year 9 students took extra maths lessons to excel in class, and 13 percent did so 
to keep up. 
Most Year 9 students had high educational expectations, with nearly three-quarters expecting 
to do some form of tertiary study. Generally, students with lower educational expectations had 
lower mathematics achievement, on average.
More Year 9 girls had higher expectations than Year 9 boys. Compared with non-Māori, fewer 
Māori students planned to pursue a university education. More Asian students, compared with 
non-Asian students, expected a university education.
Compared to previous cycles, in 2014/15, more Year 9 students expected to complete NCEA Level 
2 or 3, get a national diploma at a tertiary institution, or gain post-graduate diplomas and degrees.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Reading behaviour of Year 9 students
New Zealand Year 9 students were asked how often they spent time reading for enjoyment, to gauge their 
reading habits. Reading resources were grouped in two categories; one included books, e-books, or comics, 
and the second included webpages, blogs or other electronic media. As shown in Exhibit 8.1, the majority 
of Year 9 students (86%) read for enjoyment at least once or twice a week. About three-quarters of Year 
9 students read web pages, blogs or other electronic media, and about two-thirds read books, e-books or 
comics at least once or twice a week. Only nine percent read once or twice a month, and four percent of Year 
9 students reported never or almost never reading for enjoyment (see Exhibit 8.1). 
Reading books, e-books or comics for enjoyment appeared to have a strong relationship with the 
mathematics achievement of Year 9 students; those who read more often had significantly higher scores than 
those who read less often. Reading webpages, blogs or other electronic media also had a positive relationship 
with achievement, although not as strong as reading books, e-books or comics. The Year 9 students who 
never or almost never read these web resources achieved significantly lower than those who read them at 
least once or twice a month or more often. However, there were no significant score differences between 
those who read them once or twice a month, and those who did so more frequently.
Activities outside of school are part of students' everyday life, and play an important 
role in their development as a means of informal learning. In the home context, these 
activities may include independent reading, travelling, exploring, holidaying, and 
many other leisure pastimes. Regular leisure activities like sports, nature, art and 
cultural activities are a big part of the New Zealand lifestyle. Some of these activities 
may boost student achievement; some may provide self-confidence and a vision 
for their future. This chapter will focus on reading for enjoyment, leisure activities 
outside of school, mathematics tutoring outside of school, and students’ educational 
expectations, which may have an effect on student outcomes.

Activities outside of school
New Zealand Year 9 students were asked to report the frequency in which they participated in various 
one-off or regular leisure activities outside of school.
Exhibit 8.2 shows that high proportions of Year 9 students have been to the movies and participated in 
various outdoor activities (such as tramping, sailing, skiing, fishing, biking) more than 4 times in the year. 
Having a holiday in another country and camping were the activities that a large proportion of Year 9 
students never participated in over the year. There was no consistent relationship between the frequency 
of doing these activities and the students’ mathematics achievement.

As shown in Exhibit 8.3, New Zealand Year 9 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity,

higher scores than those in the other categories of doing sports, while these students in the other categories

Tutoring in mathematics outside of school
The lower secondary students were asked if they had attended extra lessons or tutoring in mathematics 
outside school during the last 12 months, and whether they attended to excel in class or to keep up in 
class. They were also asked to report how long they had attended extra tutorials outside of school in the 
last 12 months. As Exhibit 8.4 shows, New Zealand had a higher proportion of Year 9 students who had 
not attended tutorials outside of school recently, compared to many other countries, and the international 
average. Most high-performing Asian countries had more than half of their lower secondary students 
attending extra lessons or tutoring for mathematics either to keep up or to excel in class. Most of those who 
took extra tutorials had done so for less than four months. Asian high-achievers were the exception, where 
bigger proportions of lower secondary students had had tutoring for more than eight months. 
Perhaps not surprisingly, in most countries, those who did not take extra lessons or tutoring had higher 
achievement than those who did. Japan, Chinese Taipei and the Republic of Korea were exceptions, where 
lower secondary students who had extra tutorials to excel in class had higher mathematics achievement than 
those who did not have extra tutorials, or who had them to keep up in class. Asian high-achievers also stood 
out, with those taking extra tutorials for more than eight months having higher achievement than those 
doing so for shorter periods.

Education expectations
At the age of 14, many students will not yet have made decisions about their future. However, Year 9 students 
were asked about their intentions for further education. This can give an indication of the importance they 
place on education. Very few Year 9 students expected that they would finish school without some form of 
qualification, with only two percent saying they would only complete up to the end of Year 10, and a further 
four percent who expected to complete only Level 1 of NCEA (see Exhibit 8.5). Most Year 9 students (72%) 
expected to do some form of tertiary study and complete either a certificate, a diploma, or a degree. About 
one-third of Year 9 students (32%) expected to complete some form of post-graduate diploma or degree.
Generally, Year 9 students with lower educational expectations had lower mathematics achievement, on 
average, as shown in Exhibit 8.5. The exception to this general pattern of lower achievement for lower 
expectations was the group of students who expected to gain a PhD; these students had significantly lower 
mathematics achievement than those with other postgraduate tertiary expectations.

CHAPTER 9:
 Key Findings
A big proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students (46%) did not like learning mathematics, and 
about the same proportion (44%) were not confident in mathematics. New Zealand had similar 
proportions of confident lower secondary students as the international average; however, more 
New Zealand Year 9 students did not like learning mathematics than their peers internationally.
Despite big proportions of students not liking and not being confident in mathematics, only 
one in ten Year 9 students did not value mathematics. New Zealand had slightly more lower 
secondary students who valued mathematics, compared to the international average.
Overall, Year 9 students who were more positive about mathematics had higher average 
(mean) achievement than those who were less positive. The difference in mean mathematics 
achievement between the most positive and least positive students was larger on the Students 
confident in mathematics scale, than on the Students like learning mathematics, and the 
Students value mathematics scales.
There was no change between 2010/11 and 2014/15 when considering the proportions of Year 9 
students who like mathematics, value mathematics and are confident in mathematics.
New Zealand Year 9 boys were more positive about mathematics than Year 9 girls; more boys 
liked learning mathematics, were confident in mathematics, and valued mathematics.
The difference in mathematics achievement, on average, between boys who were positive 
about mathematics and those who were negative was greater than the difference between 
girls who were positive and girls who were negative.
Overall, Year 9 students in the Asian and Pasifika ethnic groupings were more positive about 
mathematics, compared with students in the Māori and Pākehā/European groupings.
Year 9 student attitudes 
to and engagement with 
mathematics  
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Student attitudes toward mathematics
To gauge their attitudes towards learning mathematics, students were asked how much they agreed with a 
series of 27 statements. Nine of the statements were about how much students liked learning mathematics, 
another nine were about how confident they were in mathematics, and the rest were about how much they 
valued mathematics. Students were given four response options: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; 
and disagree a lot.
Positive and negative statements were interwoven in the questionnaire, but are reordered in Exhibit 9.1 for 
ease of reading. Two statements which had some of the highest proportions of New Zealand Year 9 students 
agreeing a lot were that it is important to do well in mathematics and learning mathematics will give me more 
job opportunities when I am an adult.  My parents think that it is important that I do well in mathematics was 
also among the most commonly agreed statements, with 92 percent of Year 9 students agreeing a little or 
a lot. While a large majority of Year 9 students agreed that doing well in mathematics would help them to 
get into the university, polytechnic, or other tertiary institute of their choice (88%) and get the job they want 
(82%), only 47 percent of students indicated that they would like a job that involves using mathematics. 
Maths was not a favourite subject of 61 percent of Year 9 students, and about the same proportion (59%) 
disagreed that they look forward to maths class, and disagreed that they like any schoolwork that involves 
numbers.
Regarding the students’ confidence in mathematics, three-quarters agreed (either a little or a lot) that they 
usually do well in maths. Negative statements like maths is harder for me than any other subject and maths 
makes me nervous had similarly large proportions of Year 9 students (62% and 61% respectively) disagreeing 
a little or a lot with them. However, 54 percent of students agreed that maths is not one of their strengths.
The mathematics achievement of Year 9 students had a strong positive relationship with their attitudes; 
students with more positive attitudes towards mathematics had higher achievement, on average, than those 
with less positive attitudes.
Besides playing an indispensible role in technological advances, “mathematics 
and statistics have a broad range of practical applications in everyday life, in other 
learning areas, and in workplaces” (Ministry of Education, 2007). According to 
Gronmo et al. (2013), “learning mathematics improves problem-solving skills, and 
working through problems can teach persistence and perseverance”. 
It seems to be widely assumed that a desire to learn mathematics may have links 
with students’ confidence in, beliefs and attitudes towards mathematics, as well 
as their mathematical achievement (Peters & Kortecamp, 2010; Grootenboer 
& Marshman, 2015; Bonne, 2016; McDonough & Sullivan, 2014). In this regard, 
understanding the influence of these factors on students' learning and achievement 
seems important, and may lead to shaping the practices that support confident, 
connected, actively involved and lifelong learners, as the New Zealand Curriculum 
envisions. It may also help to increase the proportion of students choosing to pursue 
mathematics related majors as their future career. 
This chapter looks at students’ attitudes towards learning mathematics, and their 
achievement in the context of how much they like learning mathematics, how much 
they value mathematics, and how confident they are in mathematics.

Students like learning mathematics
The Students like learning mathematics scale included the following nine statements:
»»
I enjoy learning maths;
»»
I wish I did not have to learn maths;
»»
maths is boring;
»»
I learn many interesting things in maths;
»»
I like maths;
»»
I like any schoolwork that involves numbers;
»»
I like to solve maths problems;
»»
I look forward to maths class;
»»
maths is one of my favourite subjects.
The lower secondary students were scored according to their degree of agreement with these statements, 
and the results were then categorised into one of three groups: those who very much like learning 
mathematics; those who like learning mathematics; and those who do not like learning mathematics. Note 
that TIMSS 2010/11 analysis used different scale categories - like learning mathematics, somewhat like 
learning mathematics, and do not like learning mathematics (Exhibit 8.2, Mullis et al., 2012).
As shown in Exhibit 9.2, a relatively big proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students (46%) were classified 
as not liking to learn mathematics, and only 14 percent very much liked learning mathematics. Compared to 
the international average proportions, New Zealand lower secondary students were a bit more negative; the 
proportion of those who very much like learning mathematics in New Zealand was less than the international 
average, and those who do not like learning mathematics was larger than the international average.
The students who liked learning mathematics more had higher mean mathematics achievement than those 
who liked it less. The pattern of achievement was similar, both for New Zealand, and the international 
average. The difference between those who very much like and those who do not like learning mathematics 
was similar for both New Zealand, and on average internationally (58 and 56 scale score points respectively).

2014/15 were not significantly different from 2010/11 (Exhibit 10.4, Mullis et al., 2016).
Students value mathematics
The Students value mathematics scale included the following nine statements: 
»»

»»

The students were categorised into one of three groups: strongly value; value; or do not value mathematics, 
according to their responses to these statements. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 study used different scale 
categories – students who value, somewhat value and do not value mathematics (Exhibit 8.3, Mullis et al., 
2012).
As shown in Exhibit 9.3, 42 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students strongly value mathematics, and only 
10 percent do not value mathematics. New Zealand had slightly more lower secondary students who valued 
mathematics, compared to the international average.
Lower secondary students who valued mathematics more had higher mean achievement than those who 
valued it less. This pattern was similar both in New Zealand, and in many other participating countries.
Overall, there was no significant change in the proportion of students valuing mathematics between 2010/11 
and 2014/15 (see Exhibit 10.7, Mullis et al., 2016).

Students’ confidence in mathematics
The Students confident in mathematics scale included the following nine statements:
»»
I usually do well in maths;
»»
maths is more difficult for me than for many of my classmates;
»»
maths is not one of my strengths;
»»
I learn things quickly in maths;
»»
maths makes me nervous;
»»
I am good at working out difficult maths problems;
»»
my teacher tells me I am good at maths;
»»
maths is harder for me than any other subject;
»»
maths makes me confused.
The students were categorised into one of three groups: very confident; confident; or not confident in 
mathematics, according to their responses to these statements. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 study used 
different scale categories – students who were confident, somewhat confident and not confident in 
mathematics (Exhibit 8.5, Mullis et al., 2012).
As shown in Exhibit 9.4, 44 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were not confident in mathematics, 
while only 12 percent were very confident. New Zealand had similar proportions to the international average 
across all confidence levels of lower secondary students.
Lower secondary students who were more confident about their abilities to learn mathematics had higher 
mean achievement, on average, than those who were less confident. There was a relatively large difference 
of 120 scale score points for New Zealand Year 9 students at these opposite ends of the confidence scale. 
This scale is different from the liking mathematics (SLM) and valuing mathematics (SVM) scales, which had 
smaller differences (58 and 47 scale score points respectively) in achievement of students on the most 
positive and least positive ends of the scales.
Many of the high-performing countries had quite low proportions of lower secondary students who were very 
confident in mathematics (Singapore 13%, Hong Kong SAR 10%, Chinese Taipei 9%, the Republic. of Korea 8%, 
and Japan 5%). However, within each country, those students who had the highest levels of confidence in their 
mathematics abilities had higher average achievement than those who were less confident.

Attitudes to mathematics by gender
On the Students like learning mathematics scale, more New Zealand Year 9 boys liked learning mathematics 
(in both the very much like and like categories) than Year 9 girls (see Exhibit 9.5). Among both girls and 
boys, those who liked learning mathematics more had higher scores than those who liked it less.
The range of achievement between those who very much liked and who did not like learning mathematics 
was bigger for boys (74 score points) than for girls (42 score points).

more had higher scores than those who valued it less.

was bigger for boys (68 score points) than for girls (28 score points).

Attitudes to mathematics by ethnicity
As shown in Exhibit 9.8, compared with students not in their ethnic grouping, the Māori (50%) and Pākehā/
European (51%) groupings had bigger proportions of Year 9 students who did not like learning mathematics. 
In contrast, the Asian (22%), Pasifika (19%) and Other (20%) ethnic groupings had bigger proportions of Year 
9 students who very much like learning mathematics, compared with students not in their ethnic grouping.
There was, however, a less consistent pattern between the students liking to learn mathematics and their 
achievement scores across ethnic groupings. Māori and Asian students who do not like and who like learning 
mathematics had similar achievement scores, but those who very much like learning mathematics had higher 
mathematics achievement, on average. Pasifika students only had a significant difference in achievement 
between those who very much like and those who do not like learning mathematics. Only Pākehā/European 
students followed the general pattern, where students who like mathematics more achieved higher scores 
than those who like it less.

On the Students value mathematics scale, the majority of Year 9 students across all ethnic groupings value 
mathematics (strongly value and value categories combined). Asian students stood out with a higher 
proportion of students (51% compared to 41% of non-Asian) who strongly value mathematics. More Māori 
(13%) and Pākehā/European (11%) students do not value mathematics, compared to non-Māori and non-
Pākehā/European students (9% and 7% respectively - see Exhibit 9.9). 
There was no consistent pattern in the students valuing mathematics and their achievement scores across 
ethnic groupings. Pākehā/European students followed the general pattern, where students who valued 
mathematics more achieved higher scores than those who valued it less. For all other ethnic groupings, a 
significant difference was observed only between those who do not value mathematics, and those in the 
other categories. Students who strongly value and who value mathematics had similar scores.

To report on Year 9 boys and girls within ethnic groupings on these three indices, only the Māori and 
Pākehā/European groupings had enough students to compare statistically. More Pākehā/European Year 9 
boys reported liking mathematics, valuing mathematics and being confident in mathematics, compared to 
Pākehā/European Year 9 girls. The Māori Year 9 girls and boys had similar proportions across all categories 
on the Students value mathematics scale, however, more Māori boys reported liking and being confident in 
mathematics than Māori girls.
All Māori boys and girls, and Pākehā/European boys and girls, followed the same pattern of achievement as 
the general student population on the Students like mathematics and Students confident in mathematics 
scales; those who were in more positive categories achieved higher than those in less positive categories. On 
the Students value mathematics scale, Year 9 Māori boys, Pākehā/European boys and Pākehā/European girls 
followed the general pattern, as in the other scales. However, how much they valued mathematics appeared 
to make no difference to the achievement of Māori Year 9 girls, as they had similar achievement across all 
scale categories.

Year 9 teachers of 
mathematics 
CHAPTER 10:
 Key Findings
More New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers with postgraduate qualifications in 2014/15, 
compared to 2010/11 (46% compared to 35%).
About two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who had specialised in 
mathematics and/or mathematics education. Internationally, more lower secondary students 
(87% on average) had teachers with a specialisation in mathematics and/or mathematics 
education.
In general, New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers felt well prepared to teach most of the 
topics in mathematics. The topics fewest teachers felt very well prepared to teach were also 
less likely to be covered in class.
Most Year 9 students had teachers who expressed high or very high confidence in their own 
ability to teach mathematics. Of the statements listed, the most confidence was expressed 
in showing students a variety of problem solving strategies, and the least confidence was 
expressed in developing students’ higher-order thinking skills.
Compared to the international average, a similar proportion of New Zealand Year 9 teachers 
participated in mathematics related professional development activities, but fewer New 
Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers had had more than 35 hours of professional development 
in the past two years than, on average, internationally. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

“To ensure every student achieves success our schooling system must provide 
high quality learning opportunities to meet the educational needs of all young 
New Zealanders” (Education Review Office, 2015). Within schools and kura, the 
quality of teaching has the biggest influence on student achievement (Ministry 
of Education, 2016). The New Zealand Government established the Education 
Council of Aotearoa New Zealand in 2015 as a new mandated professional body 
for teachers to lift the status of the teaching profession, and publicly recognise the 
role of quality teaching and educational leadership in raising student achievement. 
Among many tasks, the council is also mandated to develop standards for teachers 
(Ministry of Education, 2016).  
To have their practising certificate renewed, teachers are expected to comply with 
the Practising Teacher Criteria (Education Council, n.d.). One criterion requires the 
demonstration of a commitment to ongoing professional learning and development 
of personal professional practice. Schools are responsible for ensuring that teachers 
participate regularly in some form of professional development, the majority of 
which occurs in school contexts.
Along with student achievement, the TIMSS study also collects information about 
teacher education, professional development and experiences. This data may 
not provide enough insight into the connection between student achievement 
and the former, as schools may have different strategies to make use of teaching 
practices and teachers’ expertise. However, it is useful to know if well-educated 
people are being attracted into teaching, whether they are being supported in their 
professional development, and if schools are actively aiming to retain the highly 
experienced teachers. 
This chapter will report information collected about the characteristics of lower 
secondary teachers, particularly focusing on their education, years of experience, 
preparedness and confidence to teach mathematics, and professional development 
they have undertaken.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Experience
Four out of ten New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who had 20 years or more teaching experience, 
and 21 percent had teachers with less than five years teaching experience (see Exhibit 10.1). On average, the 
New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers had had 17 years of teaching experience. This has not changed 
significantly since 2010/2011, where the average experience of mathematics teachers was 15 years. 
On average internationally, around one-third of lower secondary students had mathematics teachers with 
20 years or more teaching experience. Interestingly, Singaporean lower secondary students had the least 
experienced mathematics teachers, with an average teaching experience of 9 years; Lithuania had the most 
experienced mathematics teachers, with an average teaching experience of 27 years.  
Teachers’ years of experience appeared to have no strong relationship with the mathematics achievement of 
lower secondary students, both in New Zealand, and internationally.

All analysis in this chapter presents the data based on the percentage of students. However, for ease of reading, in some

Education
As shown in Exhibit 10.2, nearly half of New Zealand Year 9 students (46%) had mathematics teachers with a 
postgraduate qualification, and 44 percent had mathematics teachers with a bachelor’s degree or equivalent. 
Compared with the previous TIMSS cycle (35% of Year 9 students in TIMSS 2010/11), more New Zealand Year 
9 students had mathemtics teachers with postgraduate qualifications in 2014/15.
New Zealand had higher proportions of lower secondary mathematics teachers with postgraduate 
qualifications, compared with the international average.1 However, there were large variations across 
countries; Georgia stood out in comparison to other countries, with 88 percent of their lower secondary 
mathematics teachers having postgraduate qualifications, whereas no lower secondary mathematics 
teachers in Egypt had postgraduate qualifications. Few Singaporean teachers had postgraduate 
qualifications (11%), but nearly all other Singaporean teachers had a bachelor’s degree (87%).

How well prepared do mathematics teachers feel?
To understand how well prepared the teachers felt to teach mathematics, TIMSS asked about the 20 topics in 
the TIMSS Assessment Frameworks (Hooper et al., 2013). The answer options were: not applicable; very well 
prepared; somewhat prepared; and not well prepared. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
89
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents

As shown in Exhibit 10.4, teachers’ preparedness to teach the topics varied across the statements. There 
were quite a few topics that most Year 9 mathematics teachers felt very well prepared to teach, the highest 
of these was simplifying and evaluating algebraic expressions (93% of students). The topics simultaneous 
(two variables) equations (57% of Year 9 students), concepts of irrational numbers (62%), and congruent 
figures and similar triangles (62%) had fewer teachers agreeing that they were very well prepared to teach 
them. As shown in Chapter 4, these topics were also less likely to be covered in class. 
Some of the statements had different wording in TIMSS 2010/11, therefore we cannot directly compare all 
statements. Comparing the statements that had similar wording in both surveys, the proportions of Year 9 
students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teachv the topics appears to have decreased across most 
of them from 2010/11 to 2014/15, but the decrease was only significant for one topic: congruent figures and 
similar triangles (71% in 2010/11, compared with 62% in 2014/15).
There was no significant relationship between the feelings of preparedness of mathematics teachers and 
their students’ mathematics achievement.

Confidence to teach mathematics
Along with asking about preparedness to teach mathematics content, TIMSS also asked teachers to express 
their level of confidence in doing each of the teaching activities shown in Exhibit 10.5. Response options 
were: very high; high; medium; and low. 
The majority of New Zealand Year 9 teachers reported having high or very high confidence in doing each 
teaching activity. Fewer reported being confident in developing students’ higher-order thinking skills than 
other teaching activities, with 40 percent of Year 9 students having teachers with medium or low confidence 
in this teaching activity. Assessing student comprehension of mathematics was the activity that most Year 
9 teachers were very confident at (with very high and high combined to make 81% of students), followed by 
showing students a variety of problem solving strategies (with very high and high combined to make 78% of 
students). These statements were asked differently in the TIMSS 2010/11 cycle, therefore it is not possible to 
draw comparison between the two studies.
Internationally, New Zealand Year 9 teachers expressed less confidence on some of these statements than 
many of their peers in other countries. Fewer lower secondary teachers felt very confident, relative to 
other countries, to inspire students to learn mathematics, help students appreciate the value of learning 
mathematics, and show students a variety of problem solving strategies. Japan had the lowest proportion 
of lower secondary students with teachers who expressed having very high confidence in doing any of the 
listed activities. Generally, countries had the majority of their lower secondary mathematics teachers having 
very high or high confidence in the teaching activities.

Professional development
TIMSS 2014/15 asked lower secondary teachers about the types of professional development they had 
participated in in the past two years. As shown in Exhibit 10.6, the most common type of professional 
development in mathematics teaching for New Zealand Year 9 teachers was around content (66% of 
students) and pedagogy and instruction (63% of students). Mathematics curriculum and integrating 
information technology into mathematics were also common professional development areas. The least 
common topic was improving student’s critical thinking or problem solving skills, with only 37 percent of 
New Zealand Year 9 students whose teachers had professional development in this area.
Compared to many of the participating countries, more New Zealand lower secondary mathematics teachers 
engaged in professional development programmes. However, Ireland, South Africa, the United States and 
Chinese Taipei all had more than three-quarters of their lower secondary teachers who had had professional 
development in mathematics content. On average internationally, when compared with New Zealand, fewer 
lower secondary students had teachers who had participated in professional development activities in all of 
these areas, except improving student’s critical thinking or problem solving skills.

Within professional development, lower secondary teachers were also asked to report the amount of time 
they spent in formal in-service professional development in the last two years. As shown in Exhibit 10.7, most 
teachers had had some professional development in the past two years (86%). On average internationally, 15 
percent of lower secondary students had teachers who did not take part in any professional development in 
mathematics in the last two years; similarly for New Zealand this proportion was 14 percent. Thirteen percent 
of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who spent more than 35 hours in professional development in 
the past two years, compared with nearly one-quarter of lower secondary students on average internationally.

Initial analyses show that the amount of time teachers spent on professional development had no direct affect 
on students’ mathematics achievement. The Year 9 students’ achievement was very similar across all categories 
of time teachers spent on professional development. However, this may require further in-depth analysis, with 
multiple factors taken into account, to examine the true nature of the relationship.

94
Year 9 mathematics 
teaching and learning  
CHAPTER 11:
 Key Findings
Many New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them 
in their mathematics classes. However, fewer students found their mathematics lessons very 
engaging, compared to many other countries, and compared to the international average. Both 
in New Zealand and internationally, the more engaged the lower secondary students were in 
their mathematics lessons, the higher the achievement.
According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students spent about 4 hours on mathematics 
per week, which was the same as the international average.
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely to work in mixed ability groups in their 
mathematics lessons than work in same ability groups. They were also more likely to work
on problems (individually or with peers) with teachers’ guidance than work together in 
the whole class.
New Zealand had a higher proportion of Year 9 students with computers available for use 
during mathematics lessons, compared to other countries. 
The New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers placed more emphasis on classroom tests for 
monitoring student progress, compared to other methods. Internationally, besides classroom 
tests, the assessment of students’ ongoing work was also a common form of monitoring 
student progress in mathematics.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

The quality of teaching is a big focus for the Ministry of Education. Two of 
the four priorities for education for 2016 to 2020 are: “powering up learners, 
parents, communities and employers to influence the quality and relevance of 
teaching and learning and lift achievement”; and “quality teaching, leadership 
and assessment” (Ministry of Education, 2016). According to Anthony & Walshaw 
(2007), “many students do not succeed with mathematics; they are disaffected 
and continually confront obstacles to engaging with the subject.” They also 
point out that quality teaching in mathematics and appropriate pedagogical 
approaches can lead to improved engagement and desirable outcomes for 
learners from diverse social groups.
The TIMSS study collects a vast array of contextual data to help analyse the 
factors that may contribute to students learning and achievement. This chapter 
will report information about students’ engagement in their mathematics lessons, 
teachers’ perspectives on instructional methods and learning techniques, the 
ways student progress is measured, and the use of technological resources for 
mathematics learning.

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Mathematics teaching at the Year 9 level
According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students spent an average of 224 minutes on mathematics 
topics per week. The instructional time on mathematics ranged from 100 minutes to over 5.5 hours per week 
in New Zealand. The average international instructional time was about the same as New Zealand, at 223 
minutes per week.
Students’ perspectives on teachers’ efforts to engage them 
in mathematics lessons
Year 9 students were asked their agreement to ten statements, shown in Exhibit 11.1, to gauge their level of 
engagement with their mathematics lessons. Their answer options were: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree 
a little; and disagree a lot. New Zealand Year 9 students were less positive in their responses about how 
engaged they were in their mathematics lessons than New Zealand Year 5 students (see Caygill, Singh, & 
Hanlar, 2016). The most commonly agreed aspect of mathematics learning engagement for Year 9 students 
was knowing what my teacher expects me to do, with 89 percent of students agreeing (a lot and a little 
combined) to it. The least agreed upon aspect was my teacher gives me interesting things to do, with 40 
percent of Year 9 students disagreeing with the statement.
Overall, there was a positive relationship between the Year 9 students’ mathematics achievement and their 
agreement to the engagement statements; those who agreed to the statements had significantly higher 
scores than those who disagreed.

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in Exhibit 11.1 into the Students’ 
views on engaging teaching in mathematics lessons scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values 
were grouped into three categories: very engaging teaching; engaging teaching; and less than engaging 
teaching. Just over three-quarters of New Zealand Year 9 students reported that their mathematics lessons 
were engaging (engaging and very engaging combined). However, compared to the international average, 
more New Zealand lower secondary students reported that their mathematics lessons were less than 
engaging (see Exhibit 11.2). Both the New Zealand and the international average show that the more engaged 
the lower secondary students were in their mathematics lessons, the higher the achievement. However, for 
New Zealand, there was no significant difference between students’ mathematics achievement in the very 
engaging and engaging teaching categories.

Mathematics teachers’ perspectives on instructional 
techniques 
Teachers were asked to report how often they used certain instructional techniques. As Exhibit 11.3 shows, 
similar proportions of New Zealand Year 9 students were in classes where teachers linked new content to 
students’ prior knowledge (61%), asked students to explain their answers (60%) and encouraged students 
to express their ideas (56%) every or almost every lesson.  The rest of the instructional techniques were less 
common, with less than 40 percent of Year 9 students having their teachers use these techniques every or 
almost every lesson. There was no relationship between teachers’ use of instructional techniques and student 
mathematics achievement overall.

Mathematics teachers’ perspectives on learning activities
In addition to instructional techniques, teachers were also asked to report on the extent to which they 
used certain learning activities in their mathematics lessons. The ten learning activities, shown in Exhibit 
11.4, had answer options: never; some lessons; about half the lessons; and every or almost every lesson. 
Two of the most frequently used learning activities by New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers were 
working on problems (individually or with peers) with teacher guidance and listening to the teacher explain 
new mathematics content, with 61 and 57 percent of students respectively having their teachers use these 
activities every or almost every lesson. As reported by the teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students were more 
likely to work in mixed ability groups in their mathematics lessons than in same ability groups. Two of the 
less commonly used learning activities were taking a written test or quiz, and working on problems for which 
there is no immediately obvious method of solution, with relatively large proportions of Year 9 students 
having teachers who used these learning activities in some lessons or never in their mathematics class. 
Seven out of the ten activities were also included in the TIMSS 2010/11 study. The comparison of results 
shows no significant change in the use of the learning activities by Year 9 mathematics teachers between the 
two cycles.
In general, New Zealand teaching activities did not markedly differ from those used, on average, 
internationally. However, a big difference was observed in learning activities like having students take written 
test or quiz, and memorise rules, procedures and facts, in which more lower secondary students, on average, 
internationally had teachers use these activities every or almost every lesson, compared to New Zealand.

Use of computers
As reported by New Zealand teachers, 47 percent of Year 9 students had computers available for use during 
their mathematics lessons. On average internationally, 32 percent of lower secondary students were in classes 
where computers were available for use during mathematics lessons (see Exhibit 11.5). New Zealand Year 9 
students were using computers to explore mathematics principles and concepts, look up ideas and information, 
practice skills and procedures, and process and analyse data, with similar proportions of Year 9 students using 
the computer for these purposes at least monthly. In terms of the overall level of accessibility of computers at 
school, 12 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students had teachers who reported that students had access to shared 
computers in the classroom. Thirty-nine percent of students had teachers report that the school had computers 
the class could use sometimes, and 16 percent of students had access to their own computer at school. 
There was no significant difference between the achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students who had 
computers available to use during their mathematics lessons, and those who did not.

Use of calculators
Teachers were also asked whether lower secondary students were permitted to use calculators during their 
mathematics lessons. As Exhibit 11.6 shows, the majority of New Zealand Year 9 students had access to 
calculators, whether restricted or unrestricted, during their mathematics lessons. Compared to international 
average, more New Zealand lower secondary students had unrestricted access to a calculator during their 
mathematics lessons. More than half of the students were in classes where calculators were used regularly 
(at least half the lessons) to solve complex problems, do routine computations, or check answers.

Monitoring student progress 
According to the National Administration Guidelines (NAGs), schools in New Zealand are required to gather 
information that is sufficiently comprehensive to enable the progress and achievement of students to be 
evaluated through a range of assessment practices (Ministry of Education, 2015). Teachers were asked how 
much emphasis they placed on different forms of monitoring the students’ progress in mathematics (see 
Exhibit 11.7). The New Zealand Year 9 teachers mainly used classroom tests, with 72% of students studying 
in classes where teachers placed major emphasis on this monitoring tool. National or regional achievement 
tests were the least used method for monitoring student progress in New Zealand. Internationally, besides 
classroom tests (75%), the assessment of students’ ongoing work was also a common form of monitoring 
student progress in mathematics, with 72 percent of lower secondary students’ progress being monitored 
using this tool. 
The emphasis on different methods of monitoring students’ progress seemed to have no consistent 
relationship with Year 9 students’ mathematics achievement.

102
Year 9 students’ perceptions 
of climate for learning 
CHAPTER 12:
 Key Findings
Seven out of ten New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about their schools, teachers and 
classmates, and nearly half had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a high 
sense of belonging had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than those with less of a 
sense of belonging.
There were more Year 9 students in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that 
they feel safe at school, like being in school, and think that students at their school care about 
each other.
New Zealand Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than 
many other participating countries. Compared to 2010/11, more Year 9 students experienced 
the individual bullying behaviours.
More Year 9 boys reported they experienced bullying behaviours than Year 9 girls. More 
Pākehā/European students reported they experienced bullying behaviours than non-Pākehā/
European students. There was no difference in the proportions experiencing bullying 
behaviours between Māori and non-Māori, between Pasifika and non-Pasifika, or between 
Other and non-Other students. Fewer Asian students reported experiencing bullying 
behaviours than non-Asian. More Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours in schools 
with lower socio-economic status. 
Those Year 9 students experiencing bullying behaviours more often had lower mathematics 
achievement, on average, than those experiencing them less often.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

A large body of international and local research indicates a close connection 
between school climate and academic achievement, as well as the behavioural and 
social-emotional wellbeing of students (Boyd & Barwick, 2011; Thapa et al., 2012; 
O’Malley et al., 2012; Ruus et al., 2007; Macneil et al., 2009; Voight et al., 2013; 
Marsh et al., 2014). Students’ perceptions of school climate seem to be positively 
associated with their academic motivation, sense of school belonging, attitudes 
towards learning, and positive social behaviours and attitudes in general. Therefore, 
it is important to study the factors that influence how students feel about their 
school learning environment.
TIMSS collects a vast amount of contextual data, including students’ responses to 
questions about their school. This chapter examines students’ perceptions of the 
school climate, in the context of their sense of school belonging and experience of 
bullying behaviours within the school environment.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
103
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Students’ sense of school belonging
To examine the relationship and connection the students have with their school, teachers and classmates, 
they were asked seven positive agree/disagree statements (see Exhibit 12.1). In addition, New Zealand Year 
9 students were also asked if they agreed with a further statement, I think that students at this school care 
about each other. The response options to all statements were: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and 
disagree a lot. 
As Exhibit 12.1 shows, most New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about their school, teachers and 
classmates, with at least seven out of ten students agreeing with each these statements. The statement with 
the highest level of agreement was I like to see my classmates at school, with 95 percent of students agreeing 
(a lot and a little combined); this also had the largest proportion of Year 9 students agreeing a lot with the 
statement. The next most agreed with statements were I learn a lot at school, I feel safe when I am at school, 
and I am proud to go to this school. Interestingly though, I think that students at this school care about each 
other was the least agreed statement, with 70 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students agreeing with it.

To understand students’ sense of belonging better, the international researchers combined seven of the 
statements to create what they termed the Students’ sense of school belonging scale1. As mentioned earlier, 
the eighth statement, I think that students at this school care about each other, was only asked in New 
Zealand. The scale had three different categories: high sense of belonging; sense of belonging; and little 
sense of belonging.

Forty-three percent of New Zealand Year 9 students had a high sense of school belonging, with 49 percent 
having a sense of school belonging, and a small proportion (8%) having little sense of school belonging. 
This was very similar to the international averages, as shown in Exhibit 12.2.
The New Zealand Year 9 students with a high sense of school belonging had significantly higher mathematics 
achievement than those in the middle category (sense of belonging). Similarly, those Year 9 students with a 
sense of belonging at school had higher mathematics achievement than those with little sense of belonging 
(see Exhibit 12.2). The differences in mean mathematics achievement were larger among New Zealand Year 9 
students than, on average, internationally.

Students’ sense of school belonging by gender
The proportion of Year 9 girls with a high sense of belonging was not significantly different from the 
proportion of Year 9 boys in this same category. Similarly, there were no significant differences between the 
proportion of girls and boys in the other two categories of sense of school belonging. Year 9 girls and boys 
with a higher sense of school belonging had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than those with a 
lower sense of belonging. Interestingly, the difference between boys with a high sense of belonging and boys 
with little sense of belonging was larger, on average, than this difference for girls.

Students’ sense of school belonging by ethnicity
Reflective of all New Zealand Year 9 students, all ethnic groupings had between 35 and 50 percent of 
students having a high sense of school belonging, and less than 10 percent with little sense of belonging. 
Fewer Māori Year 9 students had a high sense of belonging, compared with non-Māori; all other ethnic 
groupings had similar proportions of Year 9 students who had a high sense of belonging, compared 
with their non-identified ethnic grouping (Exhibit 12.4). More Pākehā/European students had little sense 
of belonging than non-Pākehā/European students. In contrast, fewer Asian students had little sense of 
belonging than non-Asian students. 
In general, among ethnic groupings, students with a high sense of school belonging achieved higher, on 
average, than those with a lower sense of belonging.

Trends in students’ perceptions of school belonging – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
Comparing the school belonging statements that were asked during the previous TIMSS cycle shows that 
there was an increase in the proportion of Year 9 students who agreed with these statements in 2014/15, 
compared to 2010/11 (see Exhibit 12.5). The only exception was for the statement I feel like I belong at this 
school, for which the proportion didn’t change.

Students’ experiences of bullying behaviours at school 
A sense of security at school promotes a stable learning environment. Bullying among students is a threat 
to this environment and causes distress to victims, leads to low self-esteem, a decreased sense of belonging 
and poor achievement (Hooper et al., 2013). To find out how often they experienced any bullying behaviour 
during the year, Year 9 students were asked nine statements, as shown in Exhibit 12.6. Their response options 
were: never; a few times a year; once or twice a month; and at least once a week.
The most commonly reported bullying behaviour among New Zealand Year 9 students was being made 
fun of or called names, with 38 percent of students reporting they experienced this behaviour once or 
twice a month or more frequently during the school year. The cyber-bullying statement someone posted 
embarrassing things about me online (new in 2014/15) was the least commonly reported behaviour, with only 
seven percent of Year 9 students reporting this happened once or twice a month or more frequently.
For all statements, the mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students was higher for those 
who experienced the bullying behaviour less frequently (i.e. responded with a few times a year or never), 
compared to those who experienced the bullying behaviour at least once or twice a month. Some of the 
greatest differences in mean mathematics achievement were seen for the least commonly experienced 
behaviours (see Exhibit 12.6).

To describe the extent to which Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours at school, their responses 
to these statements were combined into the Students bullied at school scale, and the values on the scale 
were grouped into three categories: almost never; about monthly; and about weekly. This data should be 
read with caution, as the scale is based on individual behaviours and could range from a repeated behaviour 
on a weekly basis from a group of individuals, to four different behaviours, with each behaviour happening 
on separate occasions only once a month, and instigated by different perpetrators. However, this still 
indicates the frequency students experienced negative behaviours. 
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely to experience bullying behaviours at school than lower 
secondary students in many TIMSS 2014/15 countries. On the Students bullied at school scale, 45 percent 
of New Zealand Year 9 students reported they experienced bullying behaviours at school about monthly or 
more often, compared to 37 percent on average internationally (Exhibit 12.7). New Zealand Year 9 students 
did not stand out as much on this scale as the New Zealand Year 5 students, where 60 percent of Year 5 
students reported they experienced bullying behaviours at school at least monthly, compared to 44 percent 
on average internationally (see Exhibit 12.7 Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016).
The New Zealand Year 9 students who almost never experienced bullying behaviours had similar 
mathematics achievement, on average, as those who experienced it about monthly, while both had 
higher achievement than those who experienced it about weekly. Internationally, most countries followed 
a consistent pattern where lower secondary students who experienced bullying more often had lower 
mathematics achievement than those who experienced it less often. Three of the high-performing countries, 
Chinese Taipei, the Republic of Korea, and Hong Kong SAR, were the exceptions, where the frequency of 
experiencing bullying behaviours at school had no relationship with achievement results (they had similar 
achievement across all categories of the scale).

Trends in students’ perceptions about school safety – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
The questions given to lower secondary students about school safety have changed since TIMSS was first 
implemented in 1994/95. The best comparison can be made between TIMSS 2014/15 and 2010/11 results, as 
six out of nine of the statements about students experiencing bullying behaviours at school were asked in 
both cycles, and although the wording was slightly different, these statements had the same answer options. 
As Exhibit 12.8 shows, there was an increase in the proportion of Year 9 students who reported experiencing 
the bullying behaviours once a month or more often in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11 results. Although the 
changes were small, they were statistically significant.

Experiences of bullying behaviours among New Zealand Year 9 boys and girls
As shown in Exhibit 12.9, more New Zealand Year 9 girls almost never experienced bullying behaviours, 
compared with Year 9 boys. For girls, those who experienced bullying behaviour more frequently had 
significantly lower mathematics achievement than those who experienced it less frequently. For boys, those 
who almost never experienced bullying behaviours had about the same mean mathematics achievement 
as those who experienced them about monthly, but both were higher than those who experienced the 
behaviours about weekly.

Experiences of bullying behaviours among different ethnic groupings 
of Year 9 students
Reflective of all New Zealand Year 9 students, all ethnic groupings had more than half of their students 
almost never experiencing bullying behaviours, and around 10 percent experiencing these behaviours about 
weekly. Across all the categories, Māori students did not differ from non-Māori, nor did Pasifika significantly 
differ from non-Pasifika students, or Other from non-Other (see Exhibit 12.10). More Pākehā/European 
students reported experiencing bullying behaviours about weekly, compared to non-Pākehā/European 
students. In contrast, fewer Asian students reported experiencing bullying behaviours about weekly, 
compared to non-Asian students. 
In general, among ethnic groupings, Year 9 students who reported experiencing bullying behaviours about 
weekly had lower mathematics achievement, on average, than those who reported experiencing bullying 
behaviours less often.

Experiences of bullying behaviours within the different socio-economic 
status schools
As shown in chapter 7, the socio-economic status of students has a strong relationship with their 
achievement. TIMSS 2014/15 asked school principals to report on the economic composition of their school 
by estimating the proportion of students from economically disadvantaged and economically affluent 
homes. As reported in Chapter 7, schools were categorised as being more affluent if more than a quarter of 
their students were from economically affluent homes, and not more than a quarter were from economically 
disadvantaged homes. More disadvantaged schools had the opposite, more than a quarter of their students 
were from economically disadvantaged homes, and not more than a quarter from economically affluent 
homes. All the remaining schools were classified as neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged.
As shown in Exhibit 12.11, more affluent and neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools had 
the same proportions of Year 9 students experiencing bullying behaviours. More disadvantaged schools, 
however, had a bigger proportion of Year 9 students who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly, and 
a smaller proportion who almost never experienced them, compared with more affluent schools.
The different socio-economic status schools followed the general pattern of New Zealand Year 9 students 
on the bullying scale; those who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly had significantly lower 
mathematics achievement, on average, than those who experienced them less frequently.

115
CHAPTER 13:
Year 9 mathematics 
teachers’ perceptions 
of climate for learning
 Key Findings
Compared to 2010/11, more Year 9 mathematics teachers characterised students’ desire to do 
well at school as high in their school, and more thought that teachers’ expectations for student 
achievement, teachers’ understanding of the school’s curricular goals, and teachers’ degree of 
success in implementing the school’s curriculum were high in their school in 2014/15.
According to mathematics teachers, nearly two-thirds of Year 9 students were in schools with 
high or very high emphasis on academic success; this proportion was higher than, on average, 
internationally. Higher school emphasis on academic success was associated with higher 
mathematics scores, both in New Zealand, and internationally.
Less than half of the Year 9 students (43%) were taught mathematics by teachers who were 
categorised as very satisfied with their job; however 92 percent of Year 9 mathematics teachers 
were proud of the work they did. The proportion of Year 9 students taught mathematics by 
very satisfied teachers was lower in New Zealand than, on average, internationally.
The proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students who had mathematics teachers who felt 
their school was very safe and orderly was about the same in New Zealand as, on average, 
internationally. Higher mathematics achievement was associated with higher school safety.
Lower secondary students who studied in classes where teachers felt their mathematics 
teaching was very limited or somewhat limited by student needs had lower mathematics 
achievement than those who studied in classes where teachers felt their teaching was not 
limited at all by students needs. A similar proportion of New Zealand Year 9 mathematics 
teachers, compared with the international average, reported that student needs did not limit 
their mathematics teaching.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Cohen et al. (2009) define school climate as the quality and character of school 
life. They envisage a productive school climate as one where students, families, and 
educators work together to develop, live, and contribute to a shared school vision. 
They determine four essential dimensions of school climate: safety; teaching and 
learning; relationships; and environmental-structural. 
According to the Ministry of Education (2016), within schools and kura, the quality 
of teaching has the biggest influence on student achievement. The TIMSS 2010/11 
results showed higher achievement for schools that provided good working 
conditions for their teachers (Hooper et al., 2013). Johnson (2006) and Johnson et 
al. (2012) state that “a manageable workload, adequate facilities, and the availability 
of instructional materials are important ingredients to fostering productive working 
conditions and promoting teacher satisfaction” (as cited in Hooper et al., 2013). 
Caprara et al. (2006) attests that a positive school culture and environment can 
lead to greater teacher job satisfaction and self-efficacy, which in turn can increase 
student learning (in Hooper et al., 2013). “An orderly environment makes it possible 
for teachers to focus on teaching and students to focus on learning” (Robinson, 
Hohepa, & Lloyd, 2009).
To explore teachers’ perceptions of both the physical and social school 
environments, TIMSS collected data from them on aspects of their schools. 
This included data on teachers’ job satisfaction, teacher interactions, teachers’ 
perceptions of school safety, limitations to mathematics teaching and challenges 
facing teachers. This chapter presents the findings of these analyses.

School emphasis on academic success
Lower secondary teachers were asked to characterise the different aspects of school life within their 
school that contribute to academic success. The statements varied from teachers, parents and students’ 
involvement in learning and school life, to teacher’s job satisfaction, as listed in Exhibit 13.1. The teachers were 
given five response options: very high; high; medium; low; and very low.
Out of all the 18 statements listed, the aspect of schooling rated highly by the most New Zealand 
mathematics teachers was teachers’ expectations for student achievement, with 90 percent of Year 9 
students having teachers who felt that teachers had very high or high expectations for student achievement 
within their school. The more highly rated statements were generally about teachers’ characteristics and 
role in learning. The statements least often highly rated were statements about parents; more than half of 
the Year 9 students’ mathematics teachers thought that parental support for student achievement, parental 
commitment to ensure that students are ready to learn, and parental involvement in school activities were 
not high within their school.

The TIMSS 2014/15 teacher questionnaire included 11 new statements that were not asked in previous cycles, 
but seven out of the 18 statements were also included in the TIMSS 2010/11 study. As shown in Exhibit 13.2, 
quite a few of the aspects of school climate have changed since 2010/11. In particular, with the exception of 
teachers’ job satisfaction, more New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers characterised the statements 
about teachers as high or very high in 2014/15 than in 2010/11. Although teachers’ job satisfaction appears 
to have decreased since 2010/11, this change was not significant. The other notable increase was students’ 
desire to do well at school, which increased from 32 percent in 2010/11, to 52 percent in 2014/15.

The international researchers combined the responses to 14 out of the 18 statements into the School 
emphasis on academic success scale (see Exhibit 13.3). TIMSS indicators of school emphasis on academic 
success included school administrators’ and teachers’ expectations for successful curriculum implementation 
and student achievement, parental support for student achievement, and the student’s desire to achieve. 
Therefore, the scale didn’t include the statements about teachers’ job satisfaction, clarity of the school’s 
educational objectives, amount of instructional support provided to teachers by school leadership, and 
school leadership’s support for teachers’ professional development. To report the scale in a meaningful 
way, values were grouped into three categories: very high; high; and medium emphasis. As the majority of 
responses were very positive, there was no low or very low category in the scale.
As shown in Exhibit 13.3, the nearly two-thirds of New Zealand schools (63%) had high or very high emphasis 
on academic success, according to the Year 9 mathematics teachers. Compared to the international average, 
more New Zealand schools had high or very high emphasis on academic success.
Higher school emphasis on academic success, as reported by the teachers, was associated with higher 
achievement scores in TIMSS mathematics for lower secondary students, both in New Zealand and 
internationally, even though the score difference between the students of very high and high emphasis schools 
was not significant. The mathematics achievement difference between lower secondary students whose 
schools had very high emphasis on academic success, and those whose schools had medium emphasis, was 
about the same in New Zealand as, on average, internationally (53 score points in New Zealand, compared with 
51 score points, on average, internationally).

Mathematics teachers’ job satisfaction
To better understand mathematics teachers’ satisfaction with their jobs, they were asked seven statements 
about their feelings towards being a teacher, as shown in Exhibit 13.4. The answer options to the question 
“How often do you feel the following way about being a teacher?” were: very often; often; sometimes; and 
never or almost never. However, due to the very small percentage of never or almost never responses, the 
graph shows this category combined with sometimes as sometimes or less often. 
Overall, New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers felt very positive about being teachers. Ninety-two 
percent of Year 9 students had teachers who were very often or often proud of the work they do. Most 
teachers were also enthusiastic about their job (90% of students very often or often). Less than two percent 
of teachers replied never or almost never to nearly all of the statements in Exhibit 13.4. The statement I am 
going to continue teaching for as long as I can was the least frequently experienced feeling about being a 
teacher, with 6 percent of teachers who never or almost never felt this way, and with 78 percent, the lowest 
among all seven statements, who felt it very often or often.
Exhibit 13.4: New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers’ feelings about being a teacher by the frequency 
of experiencing these feelings in TIMSS 2014/15

On the basis of the responses to these statements, the international researchers created the Teacher job 
satisfaction scale. This scale combines all responses into three categories: very satisfied; satisfied; and less 
than satisfied.
As shown in Exhibit 13.5, 43 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were taught mathematics by teachers 
who were very satisfied with their jobs, and 47 percent were taught by teachers who were satisfied. 
The New Zealand proportion of very satisfied teachers was smaller than the international average 
proportion (50%). There was not a strong relationship between teachers’ job satisfaction and their 
students’ achievement. Although the graph shows small differences between the mean achievement scores 
of New Zealand Year 9 students, depending on their teachers’ job satisfaction level, these differences 
were not significant. Internationally, the pattern differed from country to country, but there was no strong 
relationship in a lot of countries.

Interactions between mathematics teachers
Teachers, as professionals, spend time learning and improving their practice throughout their careers. An 
excellent way to learn is through interactions and collaborations with other teachers. TIMSS asked teachers 
how often they interact with other teachers, as learners, collaborators, or teachers, as shown in Exhibit 13.6. 
Four possible response options for the frequency of interactions were: very often; often; sometimes; and 
never or almost never.  
The most common type of interaction was discussing how to teach a particular topic, with more than two-
thirds of Year 9 students having mathematics teachers who reported having these interactions very often 
or often (see Exhibit 13.6). Visiting another classroom to learn more about teaching was the least frequently 
practiced interaction by a considerable margin. The frequency of teacher interactions appeared to have no 
link with student mathematics achievement.

Mathematics teachers’ perceptions of school safety and 
student behaviours
To learn what teachers think about their school safety and student behaviour in their schools, mathematics 
teachers of Year 9 students were asked to indicate the extent to which they agreed or disagreed with eight 
statements on the general levels of safety they experienced at their schools (see Exhibit 13.7). There were 
four possible response options given: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot. 
Almost all Year 9 students were taught mathematics by teachers who agreed (a lot and a little combined) 
their school was a safe place, with 97 percent agreeing with the statement I feel safe at this school. The 
least agreed statement was the students respect school property, with 28 percent of Year 9 students 
having teachers who disagreed with this to some extent. Five of the eight statements were asked in the 
TIMSS 2010/11 cycle as well. Comparing the responses to these statements across the two cycles shows no 
significant change in the proportions. Although the statement the students are well behaved appears to have 
more teachers agreeing in 2014/15 than in 2010/11, this change was not statistically significant.

International researchers summarised teachers’ responses to these statements into a scale called the Safe 
and orderly school scale. This scale describes the extent to which teachers felt their school was a safe and 
orderly environment with three value categories: very safe and orderly; safe and orderly; and less than safe 
and orderly. 
As shown in Exhibit 13.8, half of New Zealand Year 9 students had mathematics teachers who agreed that 
their school was very safe and orderly, which was about the same as the international average of 46 percent. 
Internationally, lower secondary students had higher achievement in schools where the level of safety was 
higher. In New Zealand, Year 9 students who studied in very safe and orderly schools had higher mathematics 
achievement, on average, than those in schools categorised as safe and orderly. The small proportion of 
students in schools categorised as less than safe and orderly had achievement not significantly different from 
the other students.

Challenges facing mathematics teachers
To find out about the challenges facing Year 9 mathematics teachers, they were asked to rate their 
agreement with the statements listed in Exhibit 13.9 using: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and 
disagree a lot as answer options. The most common challenge that the Year 9 teachers were facing was a 
lack of time to assist individual students, with 46 percent of students having teachers agree a lot, and 46 
percent agree a little to this statement. Having too many administrative tasks was also among the most 
commonly experienced challenges, with 73 percent of teachers agreeing a lot or a little to this statement. The 
Year 9 mathematics teachers were less likely to report experiencing too much pressure from parents, or too 
many teaching hours, compared to other challenges.

The international researchers combined the responses to these statements into a scale called Challenges 
facing teachers. According to the scale, eight percent of New Zealand Year 9 students had mathematics 
teachers who faced many challenges in their teaching, while 42 percent faced few challenges (see Exhibit 
13.10). Internationally, a similar proportion of lower secondary mathematics teachers were facing many 
challenges in their teaching. On average internationally, there was little relationship between mathematics 
teachers’ reports of challenges and the mathematics achievement of their students. In New Zealand, Year 9 
students whose teachers reported some challenges had higher mathematics achievement than those whose 
teachers reported facing many or few challenges in their teaching.

Limitations to mathematics teaching
In addition to asking about challenges facing teachers, TIMSS asked lower secondary teachers to describe to 
what extent the factors listed in Exhibit 13.11 limited mathematics teaching in their classes. Response options 
were: not at all; some; and a lot. 
The most common limitation was students lacking prerequisite knowledge or skills, with 89 percent of Year 
9 students having mathematics teachers who thought this factor put some or a lot of limitations on how 
they taught their class. The factors uninterested students, disruptive students and students suffering from 
not enough sleep were also largely reported as putting a lot or some limitations on mathematics teaching. 
Students with physical disabilities seemed to be the least limiting factor; 91 percent of Year 9 students had 
mathematics teachers who reported that this did not limit their teaching at all. However, the research didn’t 
collect data on what percentage of teachers had students with physical disabilities in their class. The fact 
that not every class has these types of students might be an explanation as to why this wouldn’t be a limiting 
factor for many teachers.
Exhibit 13.11: Extent to which Year 9 mathematics teachers indicated these factors limited their 
mathematics teaching in TIMSS 2014/15

The international researchers combined the responses to six of the statements into a scale called Teaching 
limited by student needs. The statement students with physical disabilities was not included in the scale. 
The scale had three degrees of limitation: not limited; somewhat limited; and very limited.
According to this scale, and similar to the international average, 29 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students 
had teachers who reported that their mathematics teaching was not limited by student needs (see Exhibit 
13.12). Five percent of Year 9 students studied in an environment where mathematics teachers felt their 
teaching was very limited due to student needs.
Limitations to teaching appeared to have a strong relationship with student mathematics achievement; the 
more teaching was limited by student needs, the lower the achievement. A similar pattern was observed for 
many other countries, as well as being shown by the international average.

Class size
TIMSS asked mathematics teachers about the size of their classes, as larger or smaller classes can influence 
how the teacher chooses to teach mathematics topics. The average TIMSS class size for mathematics at the 
Year 9 level in New Zealand was 25 students in 2014/15. The international average class size was 29 students. 
In the majority of countries, the average class size ranged from 20 to 35 students; South Africa (46), Egypt 
(43), Morocco (41), Botswana (40), Singapore (36) and Slovenia (17) were the few exceptions. 
It is difficult to draw a conclusion about the relationship between class size and achievement. For example, in 
some countries smaller classes tend to be in rural areas where there are fewer resources, and larger classes 
in urban areas with more resources. Remedial classes may also be smaller. However, TIMSS shows that 
high-performing Asian countries have relatively larger class sizes (ranging from 30 to 36 students in TIMSS 
2014/15), while most non-Asian higher performing countries tend to have smaller class sizes (between 21 and 
27 students in TIMSS 2014/15). The New Zealand Year 9 class size has varied over the different TIMSS cycles. 
It was 23 students in 1994/95, 26 students in 2010/11, and now 25 students in 2014/15.
