130
Year 9 principals’ perceptions 
of climate for learning 
CHAPTER 14:
 Key Findings
Overall, the principals were very positive about their teachers’ role in the school’s academic 
success. They were, however, less positive about parents’ support and involvement than about 
their teachers and students. The principals and mathematics teachers had similar views on 
the aspects of the school’s academic success that involved teachers. However, a significant 
difference was observed in those that involved student efforts and parental involvement; the 
principals were more positive than teachers about these aspects.
Overall, principals’ perceptions of their school’s academic climate don’t appear to have 
changed much since 2002/03. However, a significant increase was observed in principals’ 
perceptions of teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum and 
teachers’ expectations for student achievement. 
More New Zealand schools reported high or very high emphasis on academic success, 
compared to the international average, and to most high-achieving countries. New Zealand 
Year 9 students studying in schools with a higher emphasis on academic success had higher 
mathematics achievement, on average. 
Compared to other countries, including most of the high-achieving countries, New Zealand 
lower secondary school principals were less positive about school discipline. This was in line 
with student reports of bullying behaviours at school; New Zealand had a relatively high 
proportion of Year 9 students regularly experiencing bullying behaviours. However, more New 
Zealand schools had hardly any problems with school discipline in 2014/15, compared with 
TIMSS 2010/11. Being in a school with less of a problem with school discipline was associated 
with higher achievement in mathematics, both in New Zealand and, on average, internationally.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Principals may not be part of classroom teaching, but they have a strong, indirect 
influence on educational achievement. Hitt and Tucker (2015) established five 
essential broad domains of effective leader practices that positively align with 
student achievement: establishing and conveying the vision; facilitating a high-
quality learning experience for students; building professional capacity; creating 
an effective and supportive environment for learning; and connecting with external 
partners. As part of the Investing in Educational Success programme, the Ministry 
of Education introduced a Principal Recruitment Allowance to help New Zealand’s 
most high-need schools and kura attract highly-effective principals who can provide 
the leadership needed to lift student achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
To be able to effectively lead, principals need to see the “bigger picture”, and to 
have knowledge and insight into the complex processes taking place in their school 
(Ministry of Education, 2008). 
TIMSS collected data on principals’ perceptions of their school environment, in 
conjunction with other factors that contribute to effective schools. This chapter will 
present findings on principals’ perceptions of their school, in relation to the students’ 
mathematics achievement.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
131
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Principals’ perceptions of climate for learning
Principals of Year 9 students were asked how they would characterise thirteen aspects of life at their school, 
listed in Exhibit 14.1. The response options were: very high; high; medium; low; and very low.
The statements the principals were most positive about were mainly related to teachers. The majority 
of principals indicated that teachers' expectations for student achievement (90% of Year 9 students), 
teachers' understanding of the school's curricular goals (90% of students), and teachers’ degree of success 
in implementing the school’s curriculum (88% of students) at their school were very high or high. Other 
statements about teachers had slightly lower proportions, with the lowest being teachers’ ability to inspire 
students (74% of students).
Principals were also very positive about students’ respect for classmates who excel at school, with 87 percent 
of Year 9 students having principals who indicated this was very high or high. However, they were less 
positive about other student related aspects, like students' ability to reach school's academic goals (78% of 
students high or very high), and students' desire to do well at school (73% of students high or very high). 
Compared to their responses about teachers and students, responses by the principals to parental support 
and involvement were less positive. Parental involvement in school activities, the lowest of the five parental-
related statements, had only one-quarter of Year 9 students whose school principal indicated this aspect was 
very high or high. Of the five statements, more principals indicated that parental pressure for the school to 
maintain high academic standards, and parental expectations for student achievement were high or very high 
(just above two thirds of Year 9 students), compared with other statements about parents.

Year 9 mathematics teachers were asked the same questions as principals, and responded similarly 
(see Exhibit 14.2). While percentages were similar across all statements about teachers, a significant 
difference was observed for those about students, and parental involvement; the principals were more 
positive than the mathematics teachers about the aspects of school climate that involved student efforts 
and parental involvement.
The responses of the principals across these thirteen statements, and their relationship with mathematics 
achievement, differed depending on the statement. However, the general pattern observed was, the higher 
the characterisation of the aspect, the higher the mathematics achievement.

The international researchers combined the principals’ responses to these statements in the School emphasis 
on academic success scale (see Exhibit 14.3). However, this scale is different from the same named scale 
created on the basis of teachers’ responses (see Exhibit 13.3), as the latter included one extra statement, 
collaboration between school leadership and teachers to plan instruction, which was not asked of the 
principals. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: very high; high; 
and medium emphasis. As principals had responded very positively to these statements, there was no low or 
very low category on this scale. 
As shown in Exhibit 14.3, the majority of New Zealand schools (78% of students) had high or very high 
emphasis on academic success. Compared to the international average, more New Zealand schools had high 
emphasis on academic success. Most high-achieving countries, including Hong Kong SAR (56%), Chinese 
Taipei (47%), and Japan (45%), had more schools with medium emphasis on academic success than New 
Zealand (22%). The general pattern, as observed both for the international average, and for New Zealand, 
was that the higher the academic emphasis reported by the principal, the higher the associated achievement 
scores in TIMSS for mathematics, on average.

Trends in principals’ perceptions of school climate and 
academic success
The questions given to principals about school climate were first introduced in TIMSS 2002/03. Comparison 
of the statements included in all three cycles since 2002/03 shows little change across the cycles on most of 
the statements. As shown in Exhibit 14.4, compared to TIMSS 2010/11 results, in 2014/15 more principals rated 
teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum (significant increase from 80% to 88% of 
Year 9 students), and teachers’ expectations for student achievement (significant increase from 76% to 90% 
of Year 9 students), as being very high or high in their school.

Principals’ perceptions of school discipline and student 
behaviours
As school leader, the principal plays a major role in building and sustaining an effective learning environment 
and a positive school climate. TIMSS 2014/15 asked the school principals how much of a problem a series of 
behaviours were in their school among Year 9 students (see Exhibit 14.5). Principals expressed the extent to 
which these behaviours were a problem with one of the following response options: not a problem; minor 
problem; moderate problem; or serious problem. 
The majority of Year 9 students attended schools where these behaviours were perceived by the principal to 
be a minor problem at most. Very few students (not more than 1%) had principals that acknowledged any of 
these behaviours as posing a serious problem in their school. Among the most common behaviours causing 
moderate or serious problems were arriving late at school, absenteeism, intimidation or verbal abuse among 
students, and classroom disturbance, all with more than 10 percent of Year 9 students in this category.

To summarise the extent to which school discipline and safety affects student learning, the TIMSS 
international team created the School discipline problems scale, based on principals’ views on the extent 
to which the eleven behaviours listed above occurred among lower secondary students in their school. 
To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: moderate to severe 
problems; minor problems; and hardly any problems.
Two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students (66%) attended schools that had minor problems with 
school discipline, as reported by principals. New Zealand principals were less positive than, on average, 
internationally (principals reported 31% of New Zealand schools having hardly any problems, compared to 
43% internationally). Most of the high-achieving countries had a proportion of lower secondary students 
similar to, or above, the international average in the hardly any problems category. This finding appears to 
be in line with student reports of bullying behaviours at school; New Zealand was one of the countries with 
relatively high proportions of Year 9 students regularly experiencing bullying behaviours (see Exhibit 12.7). 
Being in a school with less of a problem with school discipline was associated with higher achievement in 
mathematics, both in New Zealand and, on average, internationally.

Trends in principals’ perceptions of school discipline
Even though the statements about school discipline were also asked in previous TIMSS cycles, in 2002/03 
this question was about the frequency of a series of problem behaviours occurring in the schools, and how 
severe a problem these behaviours were. In 2010/11, the question was adjusted to only ask how much of a 
problem these behaviours were in the school, with different answer options to the previous cycle. Due to 
these differences, we are able to compare the results across only TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15. According to 
the School discipline problems scale results, more New Zealand lower secondary principals reported hardly 
any problems with school discipline in 2014/15, compared with 2010/11 (Exhibit 7.2, Mullis et al., 2016).

139
School resources for 
Year 9 students 
CHAPTER 15:
 Key Findings
Among the most commonly reported problems by the Year 9 mathematics teachers were the 
school building needs significant repair, and teachers do not have adequate workspace; at 
least 20 percent of Year 9 students had teachers who thought these were moderate or serious 
problems in their school.
New Zealand Year 9 teachers reported fewer problems with mathematics resources, in general, 
than many of their international counterparts. 
Principals most commonly reported a lack of computer software/applications affecting 
mathematics instruction. A lack of teachers with a specialisation in mathematics was also 
relatively commonly reported by principals as hindering mathematics instruction.  
Nearly two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools where principals had 
indicated that resource shortages affected mathematics instruction. 
Internationally, the less affected the principal or teacher felt mathematics instruction at the 
school was by a shortage of resources, the higher the mathematics achievement scores. In New 
Zealand, however, there was no consistent relationship between resource shortages reported 
by the principals or teachers, and the mathematics achievement of Year 9 students
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

The quality and nature of the physical environment of schools and resources have 
been thought to have a significant influence on teacher attitudes, behaviours, and 
performance, which have been directly linked to student outcomes and achievement 
(Dawson & Parker, 1998; Schneider, 2002; Buckley, Schneider, & Shang, 2004). 
Uline and Tschannen-Moran (2008) found a statistically significant relationship 
between the quality of school facilities, school climate and achievement in 
mathematics.
In this chapter, we explore the physical environment of our schools and resources 
to gain an understanding of how the teaching environment and resource shortages 
potentially impact teaching and mathematics achievement in TIMSS. Information on 
these has been obtained from the contextual questionnaires filled in by teachers and 
principals in participating schools.
Quality and availability of school buildings and resources, 
according to mathematics teachers
Teachers in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to describe the extent to which various issues created problems 
for them, as listed in Exhibit 15.1. They were given four response options: not a problem; minor problem; 
moderate problem; and serious problem.
The least problematic of these issues, in the opinions of the teachers, were that the school classrooms were 
not cleaned often enough, with 69 percent of Year 9 students having mathematics teachers who reported 
that this was not a problem. For nearly all the other issues, more than half of the students had teachers who 
rated these as at least a minor problem in their school. Among the most commonly reported problems were 
the school building needs significant repair, and teachers do not have adequate workspace, where at least 20 
percent of Year 9 students had mathematics teachers who thought these were moderate or serious problems.
Exhibit 15.1: How much New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers perceived various issues were 
problems in their current schools in TIMSS 2014/15

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in the Problems with school 
conditions and resources scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three 
categories: moderate to severe problems; minor problems; and hardly any problems. 
As Exhibit 15.2 shows, New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers were relatively positive about their working 
conditions, with a small proportion reporting moderate to severe problems with school conditions and 
resources, compared with teachers from other countries. According to the international average, the more 
positive teachers were on the scale (that is the less likely they were to report on any problem), the higher the 
average mathematics achievement of the lower secondary students. However, in New Zealand, there was no 
significant difference in mathematics achievement between those Year 9 students at each level of the scale.

Impact of shortages of resources, according to principals
Principals were asked to rate if their school’s capacity to provide instruction was affected by a shortage 
or inadequacy of general and mathematics resources, as listed in Exhibit 15.3. This question was asked 
differently from the question asked of teachers, with the answer options being: a lot; some; a little; or not at 
all. Of the resources listed, the ones most commonly seen as having an impact on mathematics instruction 
(a lot, some and a little combined) were computer software and library resources for mathematics 
instruction. A lack of teachers with a specialisation in mathematics also affected mathematics lessons of half 
of the Year 9 students, as reported by their school principals. As shown in chapter 10, fewer New Zealand 
Year 9 teachers had a specialisation in mathematics when compared with many other countries. 
Of the general resources, a lack of technologically competent staff, computer technology for teaching and 
learning, and resources for students with disabilities were among the most common resources indicated as 
hindering instruction. A lack of supplies, instructional materials, and heating/cooling and lighting systems 
were the resources less commonly indicated as hindering instruction, compared to the other resources listed.
Exhibit 15.3: How much principals perceived instructional capability was limited by a lack of general and 
mathematics related resources in New Zealand in TIMSS 2014/15

The international researchers combined principals’ responses to the statements in Exhibit 15.3 into the 
Instruction affected by mathematics resource shortages scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, 
values were grouped into three categories: not affected; affected; and affected a lot. As Exhibit 15.4 shows, 
39 percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were in schools where principals had indicated that resource 
shortages had not affected mathematics instruction, and the rest were in schools where instruction was 
affected by shortages. The principals’ responses, overall, to instruction being affected by mathematics 
resource shortages in New Zealand schools hasn’t changed significantly from 2010/11 (Exhibit 5.7, Mullis et al., 
2016). 
Internationally, on average, fewer lower secondary students (27%) were in schools where principals felt that 
instruction was not affected by mathematics resource shortages. Chinese Taipei had a smaller proportion 
of lower secondary students, compared to New Zealand, where principals felt that instruction was not 
affected by mathematics resource shortages. On the other hand, Singapore had as high as 74 percent of 
lower secondary students not affected by resource shortages. However, Singapore, compared to other high-
achieving countries, also had a higher proportion of lower secondary students (6%) that were affected a lot 
by mathematics resource shortages.
The international average shows that, in general, the less affected the school was by a shortage of 
mathematics resources, the higher the achievement scores (see Exhibit 15.4). However, this general finding 
did not hold true for all countries, including New Zealand and several high-achieving countries; the score 
differences between less affected and more affected students in these countries were not significant.

School libraries
All Year 9 students in New Zealand attended schools that had a school library. Most school libraries 
contained more than 2000 print books, and more than 10 print magazines and other periodicals. Digital 
books, magazines and other periodicals were less common compared to print ones, with most libraries 
having fewer than 500 digital books, and fewer than 5 digital magazines and other periodicals. The number 
of print or digital resources in school library didn’t appear to have a consistent relationship with Year 9 
students’ mathematics achievement.

145
Conclusion
Mathematics achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
The average (mean) mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 9 students in 2014/15 was statistically 
the same as that of the students 20 years ago, and not significantly different from 1998/99, 2002/03, and 
2010/11. Although New Zealand’s mean achievement in mathematics has not changed since 1994/95, the 
achievement of many other countries has increased. New Zealand Year 9 students’ mean mathematics 
achievement was significantly higher than 18 countries, but lower than the mean score of 17 countries, 
including all the other predominantly English-speaking countries who participated.
Was our average achievement low compared with other countries because the test was inappropriate for 
New Zealand students? When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for New Zealand 
Year 9 students, there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when 
analysis reduced the test to contain only those appropriate to New Zealand Year 9 students, the average 
student got less than half the items correct. Many New Zealand Year 9 students were working at level 4 of 
the curriculum by the end of the year, rather than level 5. Perhaps not surprisingly, those students working at 
higher levels had higher achievement.
Strengths and weaknesses in Year 9 mathematics 
achievement 
At the Year 9 level in mathematics, New Zealand students showed greatest strength at statistics questions 
(called data and chance in TIMSS), compared to algebra and geometry. This was a similar pattern to 2010/11. 
Data and chance questions, followed by number questions, were the areas of the curriculum most likely to 
be covered by Year 9 teachers, and these were also the areas more teachers felt very well prepared to teach. 
The finding of statistics as a strength is similar to other studies, such as the Programme for International 
Student Assessment (PISA), which also finds that New Zealand students perform best, on average, on tasks 
related to uncertainty and data (May, 2013).
Year 9 students were also likely to do better at reasoning in mathematics, followed by applying their 
knowledge, while showing less strength at knowing the mathematical concepts, procedures, and facts. 
There was no change between 2010/11 and 2014/15 across the mathematics content and cognitive domains. 
Most Year 9 students had teachers who expressed high or very high confidence in their own ability to teach 
mathematics. Of the statements listed, the most confidence was expressed in showing students a variety 
of problem solving strategies, and the least confidence was expressed in developing students’ higher-order 
thinking skills.
Equity among Year 9 students
“Over many years, the New Zealand education system has been characterised by relatively high levels of 
inequality” (Ministry of Education, 2016). One way to examine equity is to look at the gap between the high 
and low performers. The gap between the high and low achievers (range) for Year 9 students was wider 
in 2014/15 than in 2010/11, but not as wide as 1998/99. Six percent of New Zealand Year 9 students were 
classified as advanced performers (reached the advanced benchmark), while 15 percent were classified as 
below low performers, who did not perform simple mathematics tasks. Compared with other countries, New 
Zealand was around the middle when considering the proportion of advanced performers, as well as the 
proportion of lower performers. High-performing countries had more than one-third of their lower secondary 
students classified as advanced performers, and three percent or fewer classified as below low performers.
Back to Contents
»

The proportions of Year 9 students reaching each classification in 2014/15 were not significantly different 
from 2002/03, or 2010/11. There has been an increase in lower achievers since 1994/95, with only 11 percent 
below the low benchmark in 1994/95, compared with 15 percent in 2014/15.
Year 9 boys and girls had the same mathematics achievement as each other, on average, but boys had 
a wider range than girls. Boys’ average mathematics achievement has not changed much since 1994/95. 
Girls had a significant decrease between 2002/03 and 2010/11, followed by a significant increase between 
2010/11 and 2014/15 – back to the same level as 2002/03. More Year 9 girls reached the high and advanced 
mathematics achievement benchmarks in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. 
Equity among ethnic groupings remains a concern. One-fifth of all Year 9 students identified themselves as 
belonging in more than one ethnic grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasifika, Pākehā/European, Asian, 
and Other. There was a wide variation in mathematics achievement within ethnic groupings, with advanced 
achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings. Māori had lower average mathematics achievement 
than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasifika Year 9 students had lower average mathematics achievement than non-
Pasifika. There was evidence that this achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken into 
account.
The relationship between home life and schooling outcomes can be examined in TIMSS. Students in homes 
with many resources for learning had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than those whose homes 
had fewer resources. One in five Year 9 students regularly spoke a language other than English at home 
(most also spoke English), however, the relationship with achievement was not clear, as the extent to which 
these students were proficient in English when they started school was not gathered.
About 60 percent of Year 9 students read for enjoyment daily. Interestingly, those students that read books, 
e-books or comics for enjoyment more often had significantly higher average mathematics scores than those 
who read less often. Overall, more Year 9 girls read for enjoyment daily than boys. Within ethnic groupings, 
fewer Māori and Pasifika Year 9 students read daily than non-Māori and non-Pasifika students. 
New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely go to the movies and participate in various outdoor activities 
than other leisure activities. Doing leisure activities did not appear to have a relationship with student 
achievement. New Zealand Year 9 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity, compared 
to other regular activities. Overall, increased time spent on regular out of school activities was associated 
with lower mean mathematics achievement. Tutoring in mathematics was less common in New Zealand than 
in many countries, nine percent of New Zealand Year 9 students took extra maths lessons to excel in class, 
and 13 percent did so to keep up. 
It was clear from the TIMSS results that some schools face more barriers to helping their Year 9 students 
achieve than others. Mathematics achievement was higher, on average, for Year 9 students in schools with 
more affluent students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The difference 
in mathematics achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was a lot higher than the 
international average.
Resources available at school could help offset a lack of resources at home. TIMSS asked about the level to 
which a lack of resources at school was a problem. New Zealand Year 9 teachers reported fewer problems 
with mathematics resources, in general, than many of their international counterparts. Among the most 
commonly reported problems by the Year 9 teachers were the school building needs significant repair, and 
teachers do not have adequate workspace; at least 20 percent of students had mathematics teachers who 
thought these were moderate or serious problems in their school. New Zealand had a higher proportion of 
Year 9 students with computers available for use during mathematics lessons, compared to other countries. 
Principals most commonly reported a lack of computer software/applications affecting mathematics 
instruction. A lack of teachers with a specialisation in mathematics was also relatively commonly reported 
by principals as hindering mathematics instruction. Nearly two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students 
were in schools where principals had indicated that resource shortages affected mathematics instruction. 
Internationally, the less affected the principal or teacher felt mathematics instruction at the school was by 
a shortage of resources, the higher the mathematics achievement scores. In New Zealand, however, there 
was no consistent relationship between resource shortages reported by the principals or teachers, and the 
mathematics achievement of Year 9 students.

Student attitudes to learning mathematics
To what extent do student attitudes relate to mathematics achievement? A big proportion of New Zealand 
Year 9 students (46%) did not like learning mathematics, and about the same proportion (44%) were not 
confident in mathematics. New Zealand had similar proportions of confident lower secondary students to 
the international average; however, more New Zealand Year 9 students did not like learning mathematics 
than their peers internationally. Despite big proportions of students not liking and not being confident in 
mathematics, only one in ten Year 9 students did not value mathematics. New Zealand had slightly more 
lower secondary students who valued mathematics, compared to the international average.
Overall, Year 9 students who were more positive about mathematics had higher average (mean) achievement 
than those who were less positive. The difference in mean mathematics achievement between the most 
positive and least positive students was larger on the Students confident in mathematics scale, than on 
the Students like learning mathematics, and the Students value mathematics scales. There was no change 
between 2010/11 and 2014/15 when considering the proportions of Year 9 students who like mathematics, 
value mathematics and are confident in mathematics.
New Zealand Year 9 boys were more positive about mathematics than Year 9 girls; more boys liked learning 
mathematics, were confident in mathematics, and valued mathematics. This held similar for Pākehā/European 
Year 9 boys, compared to Pākehā/European Year 9 girls. Similar proportions of Māori Year 9 girls and boys 
valued mathematics, however, more Māori boys reported liking and being confident in mathematics than 
Māori girls. The difference in mathematics achievement, on average, between boys who were positive about 
mathematics and those who were negative was greater than the difference between girls who were positive 
and girls who were negative.
The Māori and Pākehā/European ethnic groupings had bigger proportions of Year 9 students who did not 
like learning mathematics, and who did not value mathematics, compared to non-Māori and non-Pākehā/
European students. More Māori Year 9 students were not confident in mathematics, compared with non-
Māori students. The Pasifika grouping stood out with a bigger proportion of Year 9 students who very much 
liked learning mathematics, but also a bigger proportion of students who were not confident in mathematics, 
compared with non-Pasifika students. The Asian grouping stood out with bigger proportions of students 
who very much liked learning mathematics, strongly valued mathematics and were very confident in 
mathematics, compared to non-Asian students.
Most Year 9 students had high educational expectations, with nearly three-quarters expecting to do some 
form of tertiary study. Generally, students with lower educational expectations had lower mathematics 
achievement, on average. More Year 9 girls had higher expectations than Year 9 boys. Compared with 
non-Māori, fewer Māori students planned to pursue a university education. More Asian students, compared 
with non-Asian students, expected a university education. Compared to previous cycles of TIMSS, in 
2014/15, more Year 9 students expected to complete NCEA Level 2 or 3, get a national diploma at a tertiary 
institution, or gain post-graduate diplomas and degrees.
Teachers and teaching mathematics
Blömeke, Olsen, & Suhl (2016), in their recent research paper using TIMSS 2010/11 results, found that teacher 
qualifications and measures of instructional quality were both significantly related to student outcomes. 
More New Zealand Year 9 students had mathematics teachers with postgraduate qualifications in 2014/15, 
compared to 2010/11 (46%, compared to 25%). About two-thirds of New Zealand Year 9 students had 
teachers who had specialised in mathematics and/or mathematics education. Internationally, more lower 
secondary students (87% on average) had teachers with a specialisation in mathematics and/or mathematics 
education. Compared to the international average, a similar proportion of New Zealand Year 9 teachers 
participated in mathematics related professional development activities, but fewer New Zealand mathematics 
teachers had had more than 35 hours of professional development in the last two years, compared with their 
peers internationally.
Many New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them in their 
mathematics classes. However, fewer Year 9 students found their mathematics lessons very engaging, 
compared to many other countries, and compared to the international average. Both in New Zealand and 
internationally, the more engaged the students were in their mathematics lessons, the higher the achievement.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
147
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 9 students spent about 4 hours on mathematics per week, 
which was the same as the international average. New Zealand Year 9 students were more likely to work in 
mixed ability groups in their mathematics lessons than work in same ability groups. They were also more 
likely to work on problems (individually or with peers) with teachers’ guidance than work together in the 
whole class.
The New Zealand Year 9 mathematics teachers placed more emphasis on classroom tests for monitoring 
student progress, compared to other methods. Internationally, besides classroom tests, the assessment of 
students’ ongoing work was also a common form of monitoring student progress in mathematics. 
School climate for learning
A positive school climate has been shown to be associated with academic achievement, as well as the 
behavioural and social-emotional wellbeing of students (see, for example, Voight, Austin, & Hanson, 2013). 
The TIMSS results for New Zealand confirm this finding when the climate is examined from the point of view 
of students, teachers, and principals. 
Seven out of ten New Zealand Year 9 students were positive about their school, teachers and classmates, 
and nearly half had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a high sense of belonging had higher 
mathematics achievement than those with less of a sense of belonging. There were more Year 9 students 
in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they felt safe at school, liked being in school, and 
thought that students at their school care about each other.
New Zealand Year 9 students experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than lower secondary 
students in many other participating countries. Compared to 2010/11, more Year 9 students experienced the 
individual bullying behaviours. More boys reported experiencing bullying behaviours than girls. More Pākehā/
European students reported experiencing bullying behaviours than non-Pākehā/European students. There was 
no difference in the proportions of Year 9 students experiencing bullying behaviours between Māori and non-
Māori, between Pasifika and non-Pasifika, or between Other and non-Other students. Fewer Asian students 
reported experiencing bullying behaviours than non-Asian. More students experienced bullying behaviours in 
schools with lower socio-economic status. Those students experiencing bullying behaviours more often had 
lower mathematics achievement, on average, than those experiencing them less often. 
So what did the teachers think about the climate for learning? Compared to 2010/11, more mathematics 
teachers characterised students’ desire to do well at school as high in their school, and more thought that 
teachers’ expectations for student achievement, teachers’ understanding of the school’s curricular goals, and 
teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum were high in their school in 2014/15. 
According to mathematics teachers, nearly two-thirds of Year 9 students were in schools with high or very 
high emphasis on academic success; this proportion was higher than, on average, internationally. Higher 
school emphasis on academic success was associated with higher mathematics scores, both in New Zealand 
and internationally.
Less than half of the Year 9 students (43%) were taught mathematics by teachers who were categorised as 
very satisfied with their job; however, 92 percent of mathematics teachers were proud of the work they did. 
The proportion of Year 9 students taught mathematics by very satisfied teachers was lower in New Zealand 
than, on average, internationally. The proportion of New Zealand Year 9 students who had mathematics 
teachers who felt their school was very safe and orderly was about the same in New Zealand as, on average, 
internationally. Higher mathematics achievement was associated with higher school safety.
Year 9 students who studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs were very limiting or 
somewhat limiting their mathematics teaching had lower mathematics achievement than those who studied 
in classes where teachers felt that student needs did not limit teaching at all. A similar proportion of 
New Zealand teachers, compared with the international average, reported that student needs did not limit 
their mathematics teaching.
Overall, the principals were very positive about their teachers’ role in the school’s academic success. They 
were, however, less positive about parents’ support and involvement than about their teachers and students. 
The principals and mathematics teachers had similar views on the aspects of the school’s academic success 
that involved teachers. However, a significant difference was observed in those aspects that involved student 
efforts and parental involvement; the principals were more positive than the mathematics teachers about 
these aspects.

Blömeke, S., Olsen, R. V., & Suhl, U. (2016). Relation of student achievement to the quality of their 
teachers and instructional quality. In T. Nilsen, & J.-E. Gustafsson (Eds.), Teacher quality, instructional 
quality and student outcomes: Relationships across countries, cohorts and time. Switzerland: Springer 
International Publishing.
May, S., with Cowles, S., & Lamy, M. (2013). PISA 2012 New Zealand summary report. Wellington: Ministry

Voight, A., Austin, G., & Hanson, T. (2013). A climate for academic success: How school climate

Overall, principals’ perceptions of their school’s academic climate don’t appear to have changed much 
since 2002/03. However, a significant increase was observed on principals’ perceptions of teachers’ degree 
of success in implementing the school’s curriculum, and teachers’ expectations for student achievement. 
More New Zealand schools reported high or very high emphasis on academic success, compared to the 
international average, and to most high-achieving countries. New Zealand Year 9 students studying in 
schools with higher emphasis on academic success, according to the principals, had higher mathematics 
achievement, on average. 
Compared to other countries, including most of the high-achieving countries, New Zealand lower secondary 
school principals were less positive about school discipline. This was in line with student reports of bullying 
behaviours at school; New Zealand had a relatively high proportion of Year 9 students regularly experiencing 
bullying behaviours. However, more New Zealand schools had hardly any problems with school discipline 
in 2014/15, compared with 2010/11. Being in a school with less of a problem with school discipline was 
associated with higher achievement in mathematics, both in New Zealand and, on average, internationally.
Final comment
As well as providing us with a snapshot of student achievement in mathematics in middle primary and 
lower secondary schooling, TIMSS also provides us with valuable information about how the New Zealand 
education system changes — or does not — over time, and in an international context. This allows education 
stakeholders at all levels of the education system to reflect on the different aspects examined in TIMSS, as 
part of a review of their policies and practices.

150
Sampling
Schools were sampled for Year 5 and Year 9 
separately but with the intent to minimise the 
overlap and therefore to minimise the burden 
on individual schools. They were sampled from 
pre-defined groups. These pre-defined groups, 
or explicit strata, were based on decile, level of 
urbanisation, and for Year 9 only, school gender. 
This methodology meant that the schools selected 
better represented the population of schools in 
New Zealand. Within each school, at the Year 5 
level, classes were sampled with equal probability, 
and all Year 5 students within each sampled class 
were selected. At the Year 9 level, 2 classes were 
sampled and all Year 9 students within each 
sampled class were selected.
Exclusions
Each country was permitted to exclude some 
students for whom the assessment was not 
appropriate or was difficult to administer. Countries 
were required to keep the amount of excluded 
students as small as possible, with a guideline 
of five percent of the ‘target’ population as the 
maximum. Any countries that exceeded this 
value are indicated in the international exhibits. 
The target population in New Zealand for middle 
primary students was Year 5 students and for lower 
secondary was Year 9 students.
Definitions and 
technical notes
This section gives a brief overview of the 
technical details and definitions applicable 
to this report. For a comprehensive 
description of the technical details 
pertaining to TIMSS see Methods and 
Procedures in TIMSS 2015 (Martin, Mullis, 
& Hooper, (Eds.), 2016).
School-level exclusions in New Zealand consisted of 
very small schools (less than four Year 5 students 
or less than four Year 9 students), special education 
schools, the Correspondence School, and schools 
that provide more than 50% of their instruction in 
te reo Māori. Within-school exclusions consisted of 
special education classes, special needs students, 
students with insufficient instruction in English, and 
units within schools that provide more than 50% of 
their instruction in te reo Māori.
The New Zealand exclusion rate was 4.8% for Year 
5 and 3.1% for Year 9. 
Mean, medians, modes and 
averages
There are three measures of central tendancy, 
the mean, median, and mode. The mean of a set 
of scores is the sum of the scores divided by the 
number of scores, and is also sometimes referred 
to as ‘the average’, particularly in the international 
reports. Note that for TIMSS, as with other large-
scale studies, the means for a country are adjusted 
slightly (in technical terms ‘weighted’) to reflect the 
total population of Year 5 students, rather than just 
the sample. The mode is the value that appears the 
most out of all values. The median is the middle 
value when all the values are put in order.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 9
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Scale score points
The design of TIMSS allows for a large number 
of questions to be used in mathematics and 
science; each student answers only a portion of 
these questions. TIMSS employs techniques to 
enable population estimates of achievement to be 
produced for each country, even though a sample 
of students responded to differing selections of 
questions. These techniques result in scaled scores 
that are on a scale with a mean of 500 and a 
standard deviation of 100.
TIMSS scale centrepoint
In order to make comparisons, student 
achievement scores generated in each cycle 
are placed on the same scale. The scale was 
established during the second cycle of TIMSS to 
have a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 
100, based on the mean of country means from 
1994/95. Equating is possible because a proportion 
of questions are the same in each assessment 
as for the two previous cycles. A score of 500 in 
2014/15 is the same as a score of 500 in all previous 
cycles.
In earlier cycles of TIMSS, an international mean 
was reported. However as the number of countries 
participating changed, this mean shifted so that 
it was difficult to make comparisons across years. 
Starting from TIMSS 2010/11, only the TIMSS scale 
centrepoint of 500 is reported. This is the same 
as the TIMSS scale average reported in previous 
cycles, but renamed to avoid confusion with a 
calculated mean of country means. 
Minimum group size for 
reporting achievement data
In this report, student achievement data is not 
reported where the group size is less than 50 
students or less than 10 schools. 
Percentile
The percentages of students performing below or 
above particular points on the scale can be used 
to describe the range of achievement. The lowest 
outer limit of achievement reported in ranges is 
the 5th percentile – the score at which only five 
percent of students achieved a lower score, and 
95% of students achieved a higher score. The 
highest outer limit is the 95th percentile – the score 
at which only five percent of students achieved a 
higher score, and 95% of students achieved a lower 
score. Therefore 90% of the Year 5 student scores 
lie between the 5th and 95th percentiles.
The range of achievement
In this report, the range of achievement is the 
difference between the low percentile score 
(the score at which only five percent of students 
achieved a lower score) and the high percentile 
score (the score at which only five percent of 
students achieved a higher score).
Inter-quartile range
The inter-quartile range is more stable than the 
full range and is the difference between the 
25th percentile score (the score at which 25% 
of students achieved a lower score) and 75th 
percentile score (the score at which 25% of 
students achieved a higher score).
Benchmarks
In order to describe more fully what achievement 
on the mathematics scale means, the TIMSS 
international researchers have developed 
benchmarks. These benchmarks link student 
performance on the TIMSS mathematics scale 
to performance on mathematics questions and 
describe what students can typically do at set 
points on the mathematics achievement scale. The 
international mathematics benchmarks are four 
points on the mathematics scale; the advanced 
benchmark (625), the high benchmark (550), 
the intermediate benchmark (475), and the low 
benchmark (400). The performance of students 
reaching each benchmark is described in relation to 
the types of questions they answered correctly.
Content domains
These are the content areas of mathematics, known 
as domains, covered by the TIMSS assessment 
framework. The content of mathematics at 
the lower secondary level is made up of four 
dimensions or domains; number, algebra, 
geometric shapes and measures, and data display. 
(see chapter 3 for details).
Cognitive domains
The cognitive domains are the thinking processes 
and behaviours expected as students engage with 
the content ranging from simple knowledge recall 
to higher-order thinking skills, and include knowing, 
applying, and reasoning dimensions (see chapter 3 
for details).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Test-curriculum matching 
analysis
In order to investigate the extent to which the 
TIMSS assessment is relevant to each country’s 
curriculum, TIMSS conducts a Test-Curriculum 
Matching Analysis (TCMA). The TCMA is also 
used to investigate the impact of selecting only 
appropriate questions on a country’s performance. 
(for details see the TIMSS International Database 
guide) 
Created scales for 
contextual variables
Item response theory was applied to student 
responses to questions so that clustering was 
taken into account. For example if nearly every 
student gave a highly positive response to one item 
then it did not overly contribute to the sum. Each 
respondent was then given a score which was put 
on a scale. Cut points on that scale were defined 
and descriptions provided that detailed the kind of 
responses given in the original questions.
Ethnicity classification in 
TIMSS 2014/15
In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand 
classified each student to one ethnic grouping 
using only prioritisation. In this cycle, any student 
who identified with more than one grouping was 
assigned to each of those groupings, in line with 
the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classification 
(Statistics New Zealand, 2005). TIMSS 2014/15 
classified the following ethnic groupings based on 
student responses: Māori, Pasifika, Asian, Pākehā/
European, and Other. If a student ticked both Māori 
and Samoan, they would be classified as both 
Māori and Pasifika. Using this methodology, ethnic 
groupings cannot be compared with each other 
as 22% of students are in more than one grouping 
(some are in as many as four groupings).
PIRLS
Stands for the Progress in International Reading 
Literacy Study and is being conducted under the 
auspices of the IEA (International Association 
for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement); 
managed internationally by Boston College; and 
managed within New Zealand by the Ministry of 
Education. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls
PISA 
Programme for International Student Assessment 
(PISA) is a triennial international survey which 
aims to evaluate education systems worldwide 
by testing the skills and knowledge of 15-year-old 
students. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa
School decile
School deciles indicate the extent the school 
draws their students from low socio-economic 
communities. It is calculated every 5 years following 
the Census of Population and Dwellings, and based 
on 5 socio-economic indicators for a community:

Significance tests
In this report, all the comparisons that have been 
made are tested for statistical significance using 
the t statistic, with the probability of making an 
incorrect inference set at five percent. To compare 
the means of two groups of students, the formula 
to generate the test statistics computed in this 
report is:

the means for two groups that were sampled 
independently are being compared, for example, 
boys’ achievement in 1994 and 2006, then the 
standard error of the difference is calculated as the 
square root of the sum of the squared standard 
errors of each mean:

used in these tests, which may account for some 
results appearing to be inconsistent.

where the sei are the standard errors of all the 
contributing means (e.g. all countries), sek is the

number of countries).
Standard error
Because of the technical nature of TIMSS, the 
calculation of statistics such as means and 
proportions has some uncertainty due to (i) 
generalising from the sample to the total Year 5 
school population, and (ii) inferring each student’s 
proficiency from their performance on a subset 
of questions. The standard errors provide a 
measure of this uncertainty. In general, we can be 
95% confident that the true population value lies 
within an interval of 1.96 standard errors either 
side of the given statistic. This confidence interval 
is represented in graphs by the lines extending in 
either direction from the points.
In this cycle of TIMSS, a new method for calculating 
the standard errors has been used. Therefore, the 
standard errors for previous cycles have been 
recalculated using this new method and will be 
different from those reported earlier. This new 
methodology takes advantage of advances in 
computing and uses more information about 
variations in computations.
Statistically significant
In order to determine whether a difference 
between two means is actual, it is usual to 
undertake tests of significance. These tests take 
into account the means and the error associated 
with them. If a result is reported as not being 
statistically significant then, although the means 
might be slightly different, we do not have 
sufficient evidence to infer that they are different. 
All tests of statistical significance referred to in this 
report are at the 95% confidence level.
Weighting
Due to the use of sampling, weights need to be 
applied when analysing the TIMSS data. Weighting 
ensures that any information presented more 
closely reflects the total population of Year 5 
students, rather than just the sample. The TIMSS 
weighting takes into account school, class, and 
student level information, and the overall sampling 
weight is a product of the school, class, and 
student weights.
